### Title: AI-Driven Reproducibility and Analytical Robustness in Scientific Discovery
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23845
Summary: arXiv:2610.23845v1 Announce Type: new 
Abstract: Reproducible and analytically robust research findings are essential to scientific progress, but evaluating these properties typically requires costly manual effort. We investigate whether LLM agents can help automate reproduction and robustness analysis, using a medical case study grounded in the Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) database, a widely used U.S. cancer registry underlying tens of thousands of published studies. First, we introduce SEER-Repro, a benchmark of 20 published SEER-based papers together with the data and resources required to reproduce their findings. Second, we create an AI agent that successfully reproduces 77.4% of 2,190 atomic claims, while providing executable baselines for further analysis. Third, we develop an agentic analysis pipeline to test the robustness of the medical findings by identifying analytical choices underlying each finding, proposing defensible alternatives, and rerunning the analysis to determine whether those choices affect the conclusion. Across published papers in SEER-Repro, our pipeline identifies potentially consequential analytical choices in 9.5% of fully reproduced findings and flags them for expert review. These results show how LLM agents can support scientific review by reproducing findings, systematically testing their robustness, and directing expert attention toward potentially consequential analytical choices.

### Title: CausalEvo: Causal Skill Self-Evolution for Health LLM Agents
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21016
Summary: arXiv:2610.21016v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents for health can accumulate reusable textual skills from task feedback, such as calculation criteria, measurement time points, and formula units. Deciding which skills to retain is consequential: useful rules improve later answers, while faulty generalizations propagate errors. We propose CausalEvo, a method for causal skill self-evolution that estimates the relative effects of deliberately changed skill sets while holding the model weights, feedback cases, and rule count fixed. At each evolution step, a frozen LLM proposes candidate rules, which are pooled with the incumbent skills so that the current skill set remains a feasible choice. Paired skill replacements against shared background rules are scored on the same feedback cases; least squares converts the score differences into relative skill values, and the top-ranked rules are retained, favoring incumbents on ties. A decision-loss bound separates candidate support, approximation, and estimation errors. On six MedCalc-Bench-Verified tasks with 120 held-out cases, CausalEvo achieves 60.00% accuracy, compared with 55.83% for SkillOpt and 51.67% without skills. Across the recorded training configurations, which use different optimization budgets, it consumes 55.6% fewer training tokens than SkillOpt. Gains concentrate in particular calculations alongside regressions on others, and paired accuracy intervals include zero. CausalEvo turns task feedback into controlled decisions about skill retention and replacement. Project repository: https://github.com/txbrook/CausalEvo

### Title: When is a large language model a good calibration agent? Physics-labeled prompting for budgeted engineering simulator calibration
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22379
Summary: arXiv:2610.22379v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Calibrating expensive engineering simulators requires tuning model parameters to match reference data, a process heavily constrained by computational costs. This study investigates whether Large Language Models (LLMs), conditioned on physical diagnostic descriptions of simulation errors, can propose informative parameter updates more efficiently than conventional derivative-free optimizers. We test this on a cylinder-wake Large-Eddy Simulation and a damped-oscillator inverse problem. Physics-labeled prompts are evaluated against four text ablations (removed, anonymized, padded, misdirected) and standard optimizers (Nelder-Mead, Bayesian optimization, CMA-ES, and random search). Results demonstrate a strong early semantic advantage: the first physics-informed LLM proposal improves the initial design in 23 of 25 trials. After five evaluations, the median calibration error is 21.9 times lower than physics-free controls. However, this advantage is transient - disappearing by fifteen evaluations - and inherent LLM decoder variability yields a 9.8-fold performance spread. To address this, we introduce a hybrid workflow that uses the LLM as a warm-start, passing accumulated evaluations to a conventional optimizer. This two-stage numerical continuation policy reaches target accuracy in just 9 simulator runs, outperforming the LLM alone (12 runs), Nelder-Mead (17 runs), and Bayesian optimization (>60 runs). Supported by sensitivity analysis validating the test cases, this hybrid physics-labeled LLM scheme proves highly effective as a calibration agent under strict evaluation budgets.

### Title: Eliciting Specifications: When Does Interactive Clarification Help?
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22382
Summary: arXiv:2610.22382v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: LLMs can generate Lean specifications from task descriptions, but those descriptions are often underspecified or ambiguous and may omit verifier-relevant edge cases, yielding imperfect formal specifications. We study whether an elicitation agent (EA) can improve verifier-checked Lean specifications by seeking clarification from a reference-grounded simulated developer before formalization. The agent decomposes the task, identifies unresolved decisions, asks bounded clarification questions, and uses the answers to write a refined natural-language specification that an LLM formalizer then translates into Lean. On 189 Verina Lean 4 specification-generation tasks, we compare single-shot generation with elicitation under question budgets of k=3 and k=10, allowing at most three or ten EA questions per task, across same-model and cross-model configurations. The same-model Claude Sonnet 4.6 pipeline has negative strict pass@1 estimates at both budgets, showing elicitation is not a uniform accuracy booster. In contrast, the Sonnet 4.6-to-GPT-5.6 and Sonnet 4.6-to-Gemini 3.8 Flash pipelines have positive strict estimates. The largest strict result reaches 65.6%, a +6.3-point change (+12 tasks), in the Sonnet 4.6-to-GPT-5.6 pipeline. Six of eight conditions have positive compile-conditional estimates, with a maximum cross-model difference of +4.9 points among compiling outputs. Treatment estimates also differ across the basic and advanced splits; the largest advanced-task change occurs in the Sonnet 4.6-to-GPT-5.6 configuration at k=10, from 42.0% to 50.6%. Failure analysis identifies postconditions as the largest source of degradation. These findings suggest future autoformalization systems should treat elicited natural-language specifications as intermediate artifacts and optimize jointly for semantic correctness among compiling outputs and reliable translation of clarified information into Lean.

### Title: Towards Adaptive and Real-Survey-Aligned Simulated Urban Mobility Behaviour of Generative Agents
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22407
Summary: arXiv:2610.22407v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: In existing agent-based simulations of multimodal urban mobility, agents, representing humans, choose their travel modes either using discrete choice or machine learning models fitted on structured travel survey data, or with rules explicitly written by the domain experts. Such approaches fix a priori the agents' possible behaviour space. Hence, a factor absent from variables or rules cannot affect agents' decisions. In reality, choosing a transport mode is a multidimensional decision in which objective and subjective factors intertwine and change over time. Integrating such complexity into a city-scale multimodal mobility simulation remains a challenge. Generative agents (GAs) built on large language models promise to respond to this challenge because they carry decision heuristics that travel surveys do not record. However, to the best of our knowledge, aligning their decisions with real household travel surveys remains an open question. In this paper, we build an agent-based model of daily multimodal mobility for Toulouse (France). We compare the performance of fifteen decision-makers (simple baselines, models fitted on the survey, GAs with minimal and tuned prompts, and a typed classifier) on the real household travel survey. Our results show that GAs with tuned prompts come close to the fitted models on the modal split of a simulated day without transport disruptions, but not trip by trip. GAs also react when unforeseen events occur. The recently released Jev-1.13 TypeSafe classifier gives surprisingly great results, showing that generating output text is not necessary. The results presented in this paper would be of interest to transport policy makers and service providers looking to apply generative agent-based modelling for improving urban mobility services.

### Title: From Individual Songs to Music Corpora: Evaluating Explainable Harmonic Decision-Making on HookTheory
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22416
Summary: arXiv:2610.22416v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Automatic melody harmonization is often evaluated through individual compositions or data-driven corpus models. This study examines whether an explainable harmonic decision model developed for individual songs can be applied to independently collected human annotations without changing its rules. We evaluate a frozen Explainable Melody Harmonizer (EMH) on selected HookTheory excerpts containing aligned melody and chord annotations. EMH generates chord sequences from melody alone using explicit criteria for melodic compatibility, harmonic function, persistence, cadence, meter-aware segmentation, and Viterbi-like optimization. Human chords are withheld during generation and used only for post-hoc comparison.
  Nineteen excerpts yielded 439 evaluable harmonic segments. Exact chord agreement reached 20.50%, chord-root agreement 31.66%, and harmonic-function agreement 47.62%, with functions assessed relative to the key inferred by EMH. Decision-level analysis identified 203 segments (46.24%) where the human chord lay outside the model's candidate vocabulary and 146 (33.26%) where it was available but not selected. These findings distinguish representational limitations from differences in chord selection without treating the human reference as the only musically valid solution. The study demonstrates a reproducible, interpretable approach to corpus-based evaluation of harmonic decision-making.

### Title: Really Need Recursion? Revisiting One-pass Registration with Multi-level Multi-space Contextual Guidance
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22423
Summary: arXiv:2610.22423v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Deformable image registration is a fundamental challenge in medical image analysis. Recent deep learning-based methods typically adopt either one-pass architectures that complete registration in a single network iteration, or recursive architectures that refine registration through multiple passes. While recursive methods often improve accuracy, they incur substantial computational overhead and slower inference speed. In this paper, we challenge the necessity of recursion for high-performance deformable registration and propose M2CG, a one-pass registration network with Multi-level Multi-space Contextual Guidance. It performs multi-step progressive pyramid registration within a single iteration, guided at each step by contextual cues from three levels (previous, current, and subsequent steps) and four spaces (image space, image feature space, segmentation space, and segmentation feature space). Our M2CG supports both unsupervised (without any labels) and semi-supervised (with segmentation labels for partial training data) registration, and has achieved state-of-the-art performance across two well-benchmarked inter-/intra-patient medical registration tasks with eight public datasets. Our findings suggest that the current reliance on recursion may stem from the absence of sufficient contextual cues that comprehensively characterize the remaining distance to the target alignment.

### Title: Recoverability-Controlled Safety Miscalibration in Large Language Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22425
Summary: arXiv:2610.22425v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Safety-aligned language models are expected to refuse harmful requests while remaining useful for benign ones. However, because many evaluations score only the final response, they often conflate intent understanding with response-level safety behavior. To address this, we introduce RiskCueBench, a recoverability-controlled benchmark that separates these two stages by first measuring whether a model can successfully recover a high-level intent and then evaluating its safety behavior on the same prompt. RiskCueBench contains 160 latent intents and 640 abstract non-operational surface prompts across eight risk categories. The benchmark covers 25 completed model groups and 32,000 successful model outputs with final rubric-adjudicated labels. When conditioning on pairs where both surface forms are recoverable, we find that increased contextualized specificity induces a shift toward less safe behavior. Specifically, paired bootstrap estimates show that the refusal rate and global safety score decrease by 0.175 and 0.440, respectively. Furthermore, mixed effects modeling corroborates a 0.107 increase in unsafe cooperation. These results show that the safety behavior can be miscalibrated to the surface form even after the intent has been recovered, motivating safety evaluations that report response behavior conditional on semantic recoverability.

### Title: TRACE: Transformer Residual Activation Codebook Encoding for Communication-efficient Distributed Edge Inference
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22435
Summary: arXiv:2610.22435v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Distributed Transformer inference across co-located edge devices is fundamentally communication-bound, as exchanging activations between sequence-parallel partitions dominates inference latency under commodity edge bandwidths. Existing compression schemes reduce this cost but retain a structural bottleneck: the received data must still undergo key/value projection before attention can proceed, serializing computation behind communication. This paper presents Transformer Residual Activation Codebook Encoding (Trace), a framework that addresses these limitations. Trace places a Vector Quantizer (VQ) at the post-attention residual of every Transformer layer and precomputes codebook-indexed key/value representations. During inference, exchanged activations are encoded into compact codebook indices, allowing receivers to directly recover keys and values through table lookup instead of executing the downstream computations required to derive them. By eliminating the received data downstream computation, Trace enables communication--computation overlap and reduces a key latency bottleneck in distributed Transformer inference. A hierarchical clustering further structures the learned codebook into a multi-resolution hierarchy, enabling runtime adaptation of the communication bitrate without retraining. The framework supports two operating variants: one prioritizing accuracy and the other balancing accuracy and efficiency through buffer-compatible quantization. Experiments on ViT-Base (ImageNet-1K and CIFAR-100) and ViViT-B (UCF-101) show that Trace achieves up to 1.89% higher accuracy and up to 1.27x end-to-end inference speedup compared with the state-of-the-art VQ-based distributed inference framework. Compared with uncompressed sequence-parallel inference, Trace reduces communication overhead by up to 22,624x.

### Title: Sparse Autoencoders Can Induce Local Geometry for Mitigating Catastrophic Forgetting
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22439
Summary: arXiv:2610.22439v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Catastrophic forgetting remains a challenge in supervised continual learning, as adapting to new tasks causes representation drift and loss of previously learned information. This raises the question of which representational changes should be constrained while preserving plasticity. We therefore introduce Sparse Autoencoder Geometric Anchoring (SAGA), which converts a Sparse Autoencoder (SAE) trained at each task boundary into local, low-rank constraints on representation drift. Replay examples specify where the constraints apply, while leading right singular directions of the encoder Jacobian specify which changes are penalized. The resulting geometry is input dependent and can be retained without keeping the SAE for subsequent tasks' training. We establish its local relation to latent code matching, define its spectral truncation error, and derive a conditional bound on frozen-readout drift. With replay and snapshot history, the method achieves the highest mean Class-IL accuracy and lowest mean forgetting among the evaluated ER, DER++, ER-ACE, and SGD baselines on three pretrained ViT-B/16 benchmarks, with accuracy gains of 12.15, 4.79, and 3.22 percentage points over the strongest conventional baseline on CIFAR-100, CUB-200, and ImageNet-R, respectively. From scratch results are more mixed, while rank ablations and geometry controls show that SAE-induced low-rank geometry offers useful stability-plasticity trade-offs.

### Title: DriveWorld3D Evidence Constrained 3D World Modeling and Generative Simulation for Real-World Autonomous Driving
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22450
Summary: arXiv:2610.22450v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Three dimensional modeling for autonomous driving must continuously estimate road geometry, obstacle occupancy, traffic participant behavior, and observational uncertainty under limited computational resources. Existing reconstruction methods can synthesize realistic novel views, but visual fidelity does not sufficiently characterize the geometric reliability required for driving. Generative world models can expand scenarios and predict future observations, yet may introduce content unsupported by measurements. We propose DriveWorld3D, a framework combining temporal video learning, measurement constraints from multiple sensors, and offline generative simulation. Its online component maintains a unified world state comprising road surfaces, volumetric occupancy, dynamic entities, and road relations, while separately representing physical occupancy, observability, and evidence provenance. Action conditioned future prediction and risk sensitive planning connect this representation to driving decisions. Its offline component reconstructs recorded scenes using neural radiance fields or 3D Gaussian Splatting, then expands long tail conditions using generative models while restricting the use of generated content as geometric ground truth. We specify data partitioning, sensor ablations, fault injection, paired closed loop testing, and real to simulation consistency assessment. The central hypothesis is that explicit evidence management can reduce dangerous false free space predictions and occlusion risk within a matched computational budget while retaining acceptable driving efficiency. All performance claims remain subject to experimental validation. Keywords: autonomous driving; 3D occupancy; sensor fusion; uncertainty; generative world models; NeRF; 3D Gaussian Splatting; closed loop simulation

### Title: Political Narratives in the 2024 Moldovan Presidential Election: Stance, Amplification, and Influence
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22464
Summary: arXiv:2610.22464v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Social media platforms play a central role in shaping political discourse during elections, particularly in geopolitically contested regions. We study how political narratives emerged, aligned, and propagated during the 2024 Moldovan presidential election by analyzing publicly available posts collected from X, Facebook, and Reddit between August and October 2024. Combining multilingual stance detection, coordination analysis, and causal inference, we examine both descriptive and causal mechanisms underlying online political discourse. First, we apply large language models to classify stance toward European integration, Russia, and the two main presidential candidates, revealing clear cross-platform and cross-linguistic differences in how political positions are expressed. ### Title: Progressive Misalignment Can Emerge from Benign Recursive Self-Distillation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22467
Summary: arXiv:2610.22467v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Training models on their own outputs has attracted widespread attention as a possible route to advance AI capabilities. One prominent approach is self-distillation, where a model is trained to match the token-level predictions it would have produced when given access to additional context, such as a cue, feedback, or instruction. However, the safety properties of this training procedure are poorly understood. In this paper, we present a surprising safety failure of recursive self-distillation, where each self-distilled model becomes the teacher for the next round of training. We observe that when a safe model is recursively self-distilled on its own (safe) generations while receiving an additional non-harmful cue, it becomes increasingly misaligned across a diverse set of harmful queries; we call this Progressive Misalignment. We show this failure mode in both the Qwen3 and Phi-4 model families on SORRY-Bench and StrongREJECT. Progressive misalignment also occurs in a more realistic setup, where the fixed cue is replaced by feedback from a simulated user with benign intent. Using a gradient-based method, we are also able to find benign prompts that lead to progressive misalignment when applying recursive self-distillation, showing that harmful training prompts are not necessary for this failure mode to emerge. We hope our work encourages future investigations of the safety properties of AI systems recursively trained on their own data.

### Title: Upper bounds on the number of causal pieces in spiking neural networks
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22484
Summary: arXiv:2610.22484v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: The input space of single-spike neural networks decomposes into causal pieces, and the number of causal pieces has been shown to provide a lower bound on the approximation error for some spiking neural networks. While this decomposition is applicable to a wide range of spiking neuron models, the limits of how many causal pieces can be realised by spiking neural networks are still unknown. In this work, we derive two complementary upper bounds on the number of causal pieces in feedforward single-spike neural networks. First, we establish a combinatorial bound given by the poly-Bernoulli numbers, which applies to a broad class of spiking neuron models. The bound is of order $e^{2(L-1)N\log N+\mathcal{O}(N)}$, with network depth $L$ and maximum layer width $N$. Second, for a specific set of neuron models, we derive a bound based on hyperplane arrangements. Applying the latter framework, we obtain bounds of order $\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{(4L-3)(N_0-1)}\right)$ for the non-leaky integrate-and-fire model with exponential synapses and the ReLU-type spike response model, and $\mathcal{O}\!\left(N^{L(N_0-1)}\right)$ for the leaky integrate-and-fire model with Dirac-$\delta$ synapses, where $N_0$ is the input dimension. For the non-leaky integrate-and-fire model with Dirac-$\delta$ synapses, additional temporal structure allows us to sharpen the general combinatorial bound to one that depends only on $N_0$, independently of the network depth and widths.

### Title: AuditKIT: A Unified Evaluation Spine for Benchmarking, Judging, and Comparing Large Language Models and Agentic Systems
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22511
Summary: arXiv:2610.22511v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Evaluating language models and agents requires combining benchmark accuracy, judge-based assessment, runtime performance, tool-use behavior, and retrieval quality. Differences in interfaces, metric semantics, and trace formats complicate these comparisons and can hide failures in agentic workflows. We present AuditKIT, a source-available evaluation library that records these measurements in one result representation, with direction-aware metric contracts, configuration fingerprints, and extensible metrics and backends. For agents, it provides turn-aware tool-call matching, separates parallel from sequential execution, and distinguishes verified task outcomes from trajectory-level diagnostics. Missing or unobservable evidence is marked unscored rather than counted as failure. For retrieval-augmented generation, it combines ranked retrieval metrics, judged grounding, and deterministic evidence checks. Seven case studies spanning model comparison, serving runtimes, retrieval, tool calling, and interoperability show how answer extraction, runtime behavior, and metric selection change evaluation outcomes.

### Title: Mitigating Demographic Disparities in Explanation-Human Alignment via Rationale Regularization
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22586
Summary: arXiv:2610.22586v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: We study explanation fairness in hate speech detection. High classification performance does not always mean that a model produces equally plausible explanations for different demographic groups. Using the HateXplain dataset, we introduce Plausibility-EFG, a metric that measures the largest difference in explanation plausibility across demographic groups by comparing model-generated token attributions with human-annotated rationales. We evaluate BERT, RoBERTa, and ADV-RoBERTa, an adversarially trained model, using Integrated Gradients and SHAP. Our results show that ADV-RoBERTa improves classification performance and alignment between explanations and human rationales while reducing explanation disparities across demographic groups. These improvements are observed with both attribution methods and remain consistent across training, validation, and test splits. We also show that reducing prediction-level bias alone does not eliminate disparities in explanations, suggesting that predictive fairness and explanation fairness are related but distinct objectives. Finally, our fairness-performance analysis shows that stronger adversarial regularization can further reduce Plausibility-EFG, although it may cause a small decrease in macro-F1. Overall, this work presents explanation fairness as an important objective in hate speech moderation and introduces Fair-REF, a framework designed to reduce disparities in model explanations across demographic groups.

### Title: Can LLMs predict the outcomes of machine learning experiments?
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22599
Summary: arXiv:2610.22599v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Recent advances in AI make Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) increasingly plausible. Large Language Models (LLMs) can already propose, select, and run Machine Learning (ML) experiments. However, the space of possible design choices in ML is vast. An LLM that proposes an experiment needs a reliable intuition of whether it is worth running given the timing constraints of long training runs. In this work, we measure such intuition in current frontier LLMs by evaluating how accurately they predict the impact of self-generated ML experiment ideas before these are put into training runs. To this end, we construct a dataset of 288 LLM-proposed ideas on eight MLE-bench tasks. We then prompt five frontier LLMs (GPT-6 Astra, Claude Opus 5.5, Claude Fable 5.1, Kimi K3, and GLM-5.3) to predict the impact of each idea and compare their predictions with the actual outcomes after training. We report four main findings. First, LLM predictions are substantially better than random chance but far from accurate: even the best model, Claude Opus 5.5, explains only half of the variance in outcomes (R^2 = 0.49). Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.

### Title: Three LiDARs, Twenty-Five Datasets: Cross-Source Generalization in Tree-Species Recognition
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22606
Summary: arXiv:2610.22606v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: FOR-species20K is the largest public LiDAR benchmark for tree-species classification, pooling 25 independently collected terrestrial, mobile, and airborne laser-scanning datasets across 33 species. We introduce a multi-resolution, multi-view ConvNeXtV2 model that improves official hidden-test accuracy from 79.5% to 85.0%, ranking first at the time of submission. We then examine what this score measures. Because the hidden test is species-balanced, its overall accuracy is more comparable to a macro metric than to accuracy under the public set's long-tailed distribution. Moreover, the official random split places every source dataset in both training and evaluation. A logistic probe recovers source identity from out-of-fold model probabilities with 69% accuracy, while geometric features identify the source above 90% within two widely shared species; 18 of the 33 species occur in only one source. Under a source-species-cell holdout, evaluated on 5,566 trees from eight eligible species that retain the same LiDAR-type training support in other sources, the unweighted species mean falls from 82% to 36%, and tree-weighted accuracy falls from 95% to 60%. Count-matched retraining and experiments with two alternative backbones indicate that this gap is not explained by reduced training support alone, while an external PureForest experiment shows a smaller but consistent grouped-split effect. These results establish the 85% leaderboard score as strong in-distribution performance, but not as an estimate of source-disjoint generalization, motivating balanced metrics and source-aware evaluation. The code is available at: https://github.com/JaeLee18/FOR20K-lidar-tree-species

### Title: StratFL: Countering Covert Model Poisoning Attacks with Strategic Federated Learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22626
Summary: arXiv:2610.22626v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Advanced persistent threats (APTs) are a major concern in federated learning (FL) deployments in critical sectors such as healthcare, and finance. Traditional defense mechanisms fail to contain APTs due to their intermittent attacking behavior. This paper addresses a specific type of APT called covert model poisoning (CMP) where the attacker intermittently injects poisoned model updates in a covert manner. The interaction between the FL server and a CMP attacker is modeled as an imperfect repeated game where both players cannot observe each other's actions. A novel adversarial search algorithm called StratFL is designed alongside novel belief update algorithms at both the server and adversary. Experiments are designed using MNIST and CIFAR-10 datasets to evaluate and compare StratFL's performance against state-of-the-art methods. Empirical convergence of belief update algorithms is demonstrated across various security vignettes. At the same time, StratFL is found to exhibit an improved accuracy of 10% compared to state-of-the-art methods.

### Title: Network-Wide Freeway Speed Nowcasting and Forecasting from Sparse Fixed Sensors with a Probe-Supervised Graph Convolutional Recurrent Network
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22630
Summary: arXiv:2610.22630v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Traffic agencies need current and near-future speeds on every link of their network, but their fixed sensors cover only part of it. We study joint nowcasting and next-hour forecasting of hourly speeds on all 4{,}127 directional freeway links of the Dallas--Fort Worth region from sensors on 643 links (15.6%). Historical probe-vehicle data supervise every link in training; only sensor readings are used at inference. We call this setting probe-supervised sparse sensing. We propose GCRN+, a graph convolutional recurrent network that predicts deviations from a historical speed profile. It combines a parameter-free, direction-aware kernel interpolation of sensor anomalies, used as input features and as an output baseline, with a diffusion-convolutional GRU on the directed road graph, a low-rank adaptive graph and learnable link embeddings. It scales linearly with network size and trains on a CPU. Sensor readings are emulated from probe speeds, which isolates spatio-temporal inference from detector calibration. On four held-out test days, GCRN+ attains an RMSE of 2.21~mph for the current hour and 2.39~mph for the next hour on unsensored links, against 3.23~mph for the historical average. The best baselines that use the same interpolation features reach 2.53 and 2.75~mph. Adapted recent models without these features (STID, STAEformer, ImputeFormer, MoGERNN) reach at best 2.54 and 2.53~mph; this comparison is between complete pipelines rather than architectures. GCRN+ is best in every distance band except links not connected to any sensor through the road graph. Ablations within one recurrent backbone attribute most of the gain to the link embeddings and the adaptive graph, which can be trained because probe labels exist for every link. For congestion detection on unsensored links, recall rises from 77\% (historical average) and 86% (best competitor) to 89% at aprecision of 0.94.

### Title: A Plausible Explanation Is Enough to Mislead Multimodal Judges
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22631
Summary: arXiv:2610.22631v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) increasingly serve as judges in training and evaluation pipelines, often assessing answers together with explanations. Although AI judges are sensitive to textual factors, it remains unclear whether ordinary plausible explanations can sway judgments requiring visual verification. We introduce JUPE, a visual-question-answering (VQA)-style benchmark that fixes answers while varying explanations.
  Across 14 MLLM judges, ordinary explanations that sound plausible mislead judges, with effects comparable on average to deliberately persuasive arguments. Plain justifications and chain-of-thought rationales reduce rejection of incorrect answers by 12.7 and 13.8 percentage points, respectively. We call this failure plausibility capture. Using signal detection theory, we find a significant mean shift in decision criterion toward acceptance, without a clear change in mean discriminability. Explanations sway judgments across domains, explanation generators, and judging protocols, while tested mitigations offer limited, model-dependent protection. Such shifts can inflate performance estimates when judges evaluate answers with explanations.

### Title: ToMStories: Interpreting Theory-of-Mind in Synthetic Narratives
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22632
Summary: arXiv:2610.22632v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Understanding people often requires inferring what they feel or want from how they behave rather than from what they state outright. We ask whether language models can make these inferences, and how they do so. We introduce Theory-of-Mind Stories (ToMStories), an evaluation dataset of ~6,000 short synthetically generated narrative vignettes, each paired with a multiple-choice or open-ended question about a character's emotion, fear, motivation, or emotional wound. These psychological states are conveyed implicitly (showing), explicitly (telling), or with conflict between dialogue and description. Evaluating thirteen open-weight models (1B-72B parameters), we find that the largest models match humans on multiple choice but not open-ended questions. Our analyses point to simplifications models make in both interpretation and generation of implicit psychological states, indicating that synthetic data can help us understand how models perform interpretation, but likely overestimates how well models interpret humans.

### Title: Measuring Mechanistic Uncertainty in Large Language Models via Causal Robustness
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22636
Summary: arXiv:2610.22636v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used in critical domains, where their susceptibility to hallucinations makes reliable and interpretable uncertainty quantification essential. We introduce the concept of mechanistic uncertainty, which characterizes uncertainty through the stability of the internal mechanisms supporting a model's response. We propose the causal robustness margin, defined as the minimum internal intervention required to deviate the model's output, and operationalize it using Jacobian Lens (Jlens) concept steering vectors. Across three open-source models and five question-answering datasets, we show incorrect and uncertain responses exhibit smaller margins, indicating greater sensitivity to internal interventions, and the causal robustness margin complements conventional output-based uncertainty methods. Finally, in a controlled knowledge injection experiment, correctly learned facts show increased margins after training, suggesting that causal robustness provides a mechanistically grounded signal of uncertainty.

### Title: Hard Instances on Demand
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22637
Summary: arXiv:2610.22637v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: The conclusions of an experimental study of algorithms are only as good as the instances they are run on. Benchmark libraries for well-studied problems took decades to curate, while evaluations of new problems often fall back on random instances, which are known to be misleading: they may say more about the random generator than about the algorithmic question at hand.
  We treat instance generation as an optimization problem: parameterize the instance space, take the solver's running time as the objective, and let a black-box optimizer search for hard instances. We develop a widely applicable workflow built on the Nevergrad optimization platform and demonstrate it on four NP-hard problems: satisfiability, traveling salesperson, Hamiltonian cycle, and non-permutation flow-shop scheduling. The search needs only the solver, an instance encoder, and a compute budget. Our evolved instances beat the hardest instance of a budget-matched random draw in all four case studies, by an order of magnitude or more in three. They are often competitive with long-curated benchmark sets: our SAT champion is harder (for a three-solver portfolio) than 78% of the 498 SAT Competition formulas in its size class; our 20-city TSP champions take several times longer than the hardest measured classical TSPLIB instance (229 cities); and our flow-shop champion takes 238.6 seconds of CP Optimizer time to solve to proven optimality (versus 4.5 seconds for the hardest of 100,000 random draws) while denying CP-SAT an optimality proof. The optimizer even recovers known structure from running time alone: it locates a SAT clause/variable ratio near the phase transition and finds structurally different champions for the two scheduling engines.

### Title: Diverse Reasoning Paths Matter: Symbolic Equivalence Augmentation for Enhancing RL Exploration
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22665
Summary: arXiv:2610.22665v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. However, the role of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) data in enabling effective downstream RL remains underexplored. In this paper, we investigate a key hypothesis: the diversity of reasoning steps in chain-of-thought (CoT) during SFT is a critical enabler of RLVR performance. To test this hypothesis, we introduce EquivAug, a data augmentation framework that uses symbolic equivalence transformations to generate diverse yet provably correct reasoning paths. By converting CoT solutions into formal symbolic representations, applying equivalence transformations, and reconstructing natural-language explanations, EquivAug produces training data that differs in reasoning steps rather than surface-level lexical variation. Experiments on GSM8K and FOLIO across model scales demonstrate that EquivAug consistently achieves the highest reasoning path diversity after SFT and the strongest accuracy after GRPO. Our results establish that CoT diversity during SFT is positively correlated with downstream RLVR performance.

### Title: AlphaBet: Reinforcement Learning for LLM Event Forecasting Agents
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22684
Summary: arXiv:2610.22684v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Forecasting an unresolved event requires finding relevant evidence and deciding how strongly it supports each outcome. Yet outcome-based training of Large Language Model (LLM) forecasters often begins with preassembled evidence at a single prediction date, leaving evidence acquisition outside the learning loop. We introduce AlphaBet, an agentic reinforcement learning (RL) framework that trains a single LLM policy to search, reason, and report probabilities across multiple pre-resolution dates. Each dated forecast is scored by a proper scoring rule against the eventual outcome, allowing one resolved event to supervise decisions under different information states. We decompose forecasting regret into an evidence-acquisition gap and a probability-reporting gap, and show that Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)'s standard-deviation normalization can distort the reporting target under multi-class proper scores. On GLM-4.5-Air, AlphaBet reduces the Brier score from 0.731 to 0.626 on Forecast-Dojo and from 0.612 to 0.528 on FutureX. Controlled ablations attribute these gains to multi-date training and show that they come from improvements in both information acquisition and probability reporting. Our empirical results validate that GRPO's std normalization degrades proper-scoring-rule performance while leaving top-outcome accuracy largely unchanged.

### Title: Explore Separately, Adopt Selectively: Execution-Guided Tool Use over Large Tool Libraries
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22688
Summary: arXiv:2610.22688v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large language model agents must discover relevant tools from large libraries, e.g., thousands of tools, as new needs arise during task execution. When several candidates could satisfy the same need, execution may be required to identify the relevant one. Investigating candidates in a shared trajectory can leave alternatives unexplored or allow failed attempts to influence subsequent reasoning. To address this, we present BRIA (BRanch-Inspect-Adopt), a simple yet effective agent that temporarily isolates candidate investigations in separate branches. Each branch retrieves and executes tools to investigate its candidate; a task agent inspects the resulting execution evidence and adopts only useful results into its continuing trajectory. BRIA retrieves additional tools as new capability needs arise during task execution. We evaluate BRIA on PlanBench-XL and MCP-Atlas using a shared pool of 1897 tools, three language models, and static and adaptive retrieval baselines. Across all settings, BRIA matches or exceeds the strongest retrieval baseline. On MCP-Atlas, it achieves an average claim score of 89.77, compared with 75.40 for MCP-Zero, a gain of 14.37 percentage points. BRIA also lies on the Pareto frontier for task performance and input-token cost.

### Title: NoveltyScope: A Multi-Agent Research Assistant for Evidence-Grounded Novelty Assessment of Scientific Papers
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22703
Summary: arXiv:2610.22703v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: NoveltyScope is a prototype that compares a scientific paper with retrieved prior work and produces a structured report and a five-level novelty score. The system extracts the core task and at most two author-claimed contributions, then searches a closed local corpus using BM25 and dense retrieval with reciprocal rank fusion. Contribution comparisons request paired passages for potential refutations, and quotations are checked against cached source text. Four scoring roles (Prosecutor, Defender, Judge, and Critic) use the resulting evidence, with at most one revision of the Judge's score. Evaluation uses 50 class-balanced RINoBench samples whose idea-level labels are retained for full-paper input. Mean absolute error is 1.48 and 1.26 for single-LLM and multi-agent scoring with Gemini, respectively, and 1.22 and 1.16 with Qwen3. The vector-similarity baseline has an error of 1.68. A HiCLIP case study illustrates the contribution comparisons and paired evidence passages. Overall, the results demonstrate the feasibility of evidence-grounded novelty assessment and show that the multi-agent configurations achieve lower scoring errors than their single-LLM counterparts across both evaluated models.

### Title: ARBOR: Locating Reasoning Failures in Long Chains of Thought through Reasoning State Tracking
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22717
Summary: arXiv:2610.22717v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large reasoning models solve complex problems through long chains of thought, in which prior work has documented failure modes such as overthinking, underthinking, and unsupported deductions. Existing methods detect these failures from local signals: textual similarity to nearby steps, switch tokens, or an LLM's judgment of a step's correctness. However, what a step contributes to the solution depends on the state that earlier steps have established, so identifying failures requires a global view that tracks that state across the trajectory. Therefore, we propose ARBOR, a reasoning-failure attribution framework that represents each trajectory as a tree of reasoning states, built as each step creates, verifies, prunes, or revisits a state, and attributes each failure to a relation between a step and the state preceding it. Using ARBOR, we refine the three failure modes into distinct subtypes that harm reasoning through different mechanisms and at different costs. We find that the tree state ARBOR maintains before a step separates failure steps from the at-risk steps at which the failure did not occur. We discuss how ARBOR integrates with existing mitigations at inference time and in training data. Code, data and interactive examples are available at https://inklab.usc.edu/ARBOR.

### Title: OrderFlowDiT: Transport-Regularized Diffusion Transformer for Probabilistic Limit-Order Supply Forecasting
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22727
Summary: arXiv:2610.22727v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: We study the conditional distribution of limit-order volume submitted over the next five minutes, given the current order book and recent order flow. The task requires capturing price placement, total volume, and predictive uncertainty jointly. Coordinate-wise diffusion losses do not explicitly account for price displacement, while normalizing fields to unit mass removes information about total volume. Agreement on selected summary statistics also does not establish fidelity to the joint field distribution. We introduce OrderFlowDiT, a conditional Diffusion Transformer for unnormalized bid and ask volume-at-price fields, trained with an unbalanced optimal-transport (UOT) auxiliary loss. We also develop a full-field evaluation framework that combines a transport cost sensitive to price placement and total volume with proper scoring rules and calibration diagnostics. In the primary comparison on 1950 validation conditions across 30 U.S. equities, adding UOT reduces full-field transport cost by 45.8% and the transport energy score by 26.1% relative to training with mean squared error alone. Both measures improve for every stock. These findings support incorporating price geometry and volume into generative modeling, with field accuracy and predictive uncertainty evaluated together.

### Title: Next Token or Vector? Learning How Far to Decode
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22788
Summary: arXiv:2610.22788v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Standard autoregressive decoding advances one token at a time, even when several upcoming tokens can be predicted together. We propose CADENCE (Context-Adaptive DEcoding), a generation method that learns both what to draft and how far to draft. Gaussian prediction heads represent future spans as continuous latent vectors, and a shared decoder converts the selected vector into token predictions in parallel. A learned router selects the prediction length from the model's own hidden states and predicted Gaussian parameters, adapting generation granularity to the current context with single-token generation as a fallback. The backbone verifies each proposed continuation and retains the prefix that matches its greedy predictions. Experiments with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct on Alpaca, GSM8K, and HumanEval show that router-selected positions yield higher draft acceptance than applying the corresponding prediction length everywhere. CADENCE achieves up to 1.3x higher generation throughput than standard greedy decoding.

### Title: Self-Evolving Coding Agents via Harness-Policy Optimization
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22791
Summary: arXiv:2610.22791v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents have shown promising potential in coding tasks, and their performance is jointly determined by the policy model and the harness that mediates their interaction with the environment. Indeed, harness and policy are mutually reinforcing and evolving only one component is suboptimal---a harness tuned for one checkpoint may not target the failure modes of an updated policy, while policy learning remains limited to the trajectories elicited by a fixed harness. In light of this, we propose Self-HPO, a recursive self-improving framework for coding agents that alternates harness and policy optimization, using the evolving policy as its own harness proposer. Specifically, in each round, the current policy first analyzes its own failures and proposes new harness candidates (H-step). The policy is then updated through reinforcement learning (RL) under the selected harness (P-step), and hard tasks identified during training form the next harness search set, keeping harness optimization targeted to the policy's evolving failure modes. Across three coding benchmarks, Self-HPO consistently outperforms harness-only and policy-only baselines, improving Qwen3.5-35B-A3B's average resolve rate from 57.8% to 64.8%, with gains generalizing to out-of-distribution settings. Further analysis shows that both optimized components retain gains even when paired with their base counterparts, while their joint gain exceeds the sum of their individual gains. Self-HPO also improves reliability (Pass@1) and expands the capability frontier (Pass@k) over the base agent. Additionally, we show that with a stronger external model (e.g., Opus 4.8) as the harness proposer, the same framework yields a further performance boost.

### Title: Prune the Leaky Neurons: Mitigating Private Data Leakage in Fine-Tuned LLMs via Joint-Embedding-Guided Pruning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22792
Summary: arXiv:2610.22792v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) on proprietary records enables domain adaptation but introduces two challenges: (i) privacy leakage of memorized private data, such as personally identifiable information (PII), and (ii) balancing private data removal with utility preservation. Existing defenses, including data sanitization and differential privacy, often degrade performance, while privacy-neuron editing lacks a PII-free reference to distinguish identity-specific neurons from those supporting useful text. We propose JEPA-Prune, a post-hoc neuron pruning framework guided by a model trained exclusively on PII-free views. Alongside the fine-tuned private model, JEPA-Prune, trains a public model on surrogate and masked views of the same records using a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), capturing domain knowledge without identifiers. A cross-model representation probe quantifies deviations between private representations of leaked records and this PII-free reference. Integrated gradients and a scale-invariant specificity score then identify PII-responsive neurons, which are pruned without retraining. Experiments show that pruning just 1.6% of neurons reduces PII inference accuracy by up to 7.4x and PII extraction recall by up to 13.9x, while keeping unseen-text perplexity within 16% of that of the unpruned model for most LLMs. In contrast, privacy-neuron editing increases perplexity by at least 26x.

### Title: STRIDE: Evaluating Spatiotemporal Reasoning in Driving Edge Cases
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22795
Summary: arXiv:2610.22795v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: In autonomous driving, perceiving surrounding objects is not enough: failing to understand how they move and interact over time can lead to dangerous decisions. Evaluating vision-language models (VLMs) therefore requires testing whether they connect past spatial observations to future consequences. Yet existing benchmarks often assess edge cases through basic perception questions or test temporal understanding in simple scenarios, leaving complex spatiotemporal reasoning underexplored. We introduce STRIDE (SpatioTemporal Reasoning In Driving Edge Cases), an object-centric benchmark for long-tail, safety-critical driving scenarios. STRIDE covers six task families: spatial perception, spatial understanding, temporal memory, temporal extrapolation, trajectory prediction, and scene-context awareness. It pairs multiple-choice questions probing individual spatiotemporal judgments with related free-form visual question answering (VQA) requiring detailed scene explanations and justifications of driving decisions. We prioritize scenes with unusual object behaviors and complex traffic interactions, generate reference answers from structured dataset annotations through automated procedures, and use limited language-model assistance and human verification to ensure annotation quality. Across 12 proprietary, open-weight, and driving-specialized models, even the best performer, GPT-6-Astra, achieves only 43.4% average multiple-choice accuracy, versus an approximately 20% random-choice baseline. Further analysis reveals that strong performance in describing driving scenes does not necessarily translate to accurate safety-critical judgments, and that models struggle more with reconstructing object motion than recalling past positions. STRIDE thus exposes spatiotemporal driving limitations that aggregate VQA scores can conceal.

### Title: The Reliance Hypothesis: Chain-of-Thought Faithfulness Rises as Language Resource Level Falls
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22841
Summary: arXiv:2610.22841v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Chain-of-Thought (CoT) faithfulness matters for monitoring, explaining and safely deploying large language models, yet it has been studied mostly in English. Languages differ in level, the amount of text available to learn from, so models know less in lower-resource languages, which may change how much they rely on their reasoning. To our knowledge, we are the first to relate CoT faithfulness to both resource level and task type (knowledge- or reasoning-intensive). On parallel multiple-choice questions from Multilingual Massive Multitask Language Understanding (MMMLU) in eight languages, faithfulness rises as resource level falls on knowledge-intensive tasks but not on reasoning-intensive ones, a gap that points the same way across four models and several faithfulness measures. We propose, and find support for, the Reliance Hypothesis: models rely more on their reasoning when they cannot recall the answer, as in lower-resource languages on knowledge-intensive tasks. Model confidence partly explains the effect of resource level, but not the knowledge-reasoning gap. Languages in which models are less confident rely more on reasoning, yet less confident answers within a language do not, a Simpson's paradox. In lower-resource languages, models rely on their reasoning for more answers, not more strongly for each. CoT monitoring may therefore be most informative in lower-resource languages, where it is least often validated.

### Title: Sample-Efficient Reinforcement Learning for Policy Improvement via Flow-Reversed Action Anchors
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22852
Summary: arXiv:2610.22852v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Generalist robot policies offer strong behavioral priors, but achieving deployment-level reliability and throughput remains difficult. Reinforcement learning (RL) can improve these policies, yet its effectiveness is constrained by the cost of online interaction. Existing methods adapt frozen flow-matching policies by treating noise space as a continuous action space. We show that this behaviorally unstructured space makes exploration inefficient, often preventing these methods from extracting meaningful learning signals on challenging tasks. We introduce AnchorQ, which enables coarse-to-fine policy improvement through a structured, refinable latent action representation. At each decision step, AnchorQ uses flow reversal to map reference behaviors to state-conditioned latent anchors, samples local particles around those anchors, and applies value-guided sampling to explore promising refinements. Our analysis shows that anchors reduce identification complexity at the cost of support bias; particle refinement trades this bias against action-class complexity; and critic accuracy determines the reliability of KL-regularized, value-guided selection. Across simulated and real-world manipulation tasks, AnchorQ outperforms state-of-the-art post-training baselines in sample efficiency, delivering a 37% improvement in real-world success rate and 97% higher simulated task throughput under the same interaction budget. Additional visualizations are available on our website https://anchorq-rl.com/.

### Title: Uncertainty-Aware Budget Allocation for Efficient LLM Tree Search
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22858
Summary: arXiv:2610.22858v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Tree search enhances multi-step reasoning in large language models (LLMs), yet prevailing methods expand every step with a fixed budget, irrespective of the marginal utility of expansion. We first show, across existing search algorithms, that continuations sampled from a shared intermediate state are largely duplicates and collapse to a few distinct answers. Critically, we find that the semantic dispersion of sibling actions predicts the redundancy of their downstream continuations, permitting a subtree's budget to be fixed before expansion. An information-theoretic analysis motivates this: treating the per-step budget as the Monte Carlo sample size for estimating a step's residual answer entropy, we derive a sufficient budget that shrinks with sibling dispersion. We accordingly propose $\textbf{Uncertainty-Aware Allocation}$ (UAA), a training-free framework that decouples value from uncertainty: values determine which candidates are promising, while uncertainty, estimated from sibling dispersion at no additional model cost, determines how many merit expansion, via uncertainty-aware pruning and uncertainty-aware budgeting. Across mathematical reasoning, logical reasoning, and planning benchmarks, UAA reduces token usage by up to $90\%$ and latency by up to $9\times$ at comparable accuracy, generalizing across four tree search algorithms and both open- and closed-source LLMs.

### Title: Cooperation Without Adaptation: LLM Agents in a Collective-Risk Dilemma
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22882
Summary: arXiv:2610.22882v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Language-model agents are increasingly deployed to act on behalf of people in settings where individual decisions shape shared outcomes. We examine whether five production models adapt their contributions to changing risks and partner behaviour, or instead follow a relatively fixed strategy. In a collective-risk dilemma game (CRG), six players contribute to a shared pool over ten rounds; if the pool falls short of its target, a catastrophe may destroy their remaining resources. Four models contributed approximately an equal share at every positive risk level, and four of the five also contributed when risk was zero, even though contributing could not improve their payoff. Explicitly stating that only the agent's own payoff mattered reduced zero-risk contributions for most models, while a more neutral prompt did not. Several models correctly identified which action offered the higher expected payoff at different risk levels, yet their contributions changed little. Against fixed partners, no model consistently chose the payoff-maximising response, and in mixed-model groups, a single agent's shortfall in the final round could cause an otherwise successful group to fail. Payoff-based selection also depended on population composition: in a single six-agent group, it favoured the model that contributed least and reduced group success, whereas in larger, randomly mixed populations, it favoured an exact fair-share model at moderate and high risk. These findings show that cooperative self-play alone is not evidence of strategic adaptability. Consistent with a machine-behaviour approach, evaluations of agents in multi-agent systems should test how they respond to incentives, partner actions, and population composition.

### Title: Learning to Be Guided: Shaping Latent Geometry for Classifier-Free Guidance
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22885
Summary: arXiv:2610.22885v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) is a standard component of conditional diffusion models, and guided generation performance has become the \textit{de facto} measure of state-of-the-art models. Recent work on latent diffusion models seeks to learn latent spaces that are easier to model with diffusion. However, under CFG, generation additionally depends on how conditions are arranged in the latent space. We study this arrangement under discrete class conditions, termed \emph{class geometry}, and investigate what class geometry allows a latent space to work well with CFG. Under a Gaussian class model, we show that shifting variance from intra- to inter-class at fixed total variance makes classes more distinguishable under noise, and derive a noisy posterior objective whose per-sample descent direction is proportional to the oracle CFG correction. Based on the analysis, we propose Prototype Regularization (PR), a lightweight surrogate for the intractable noisy posterior objective, which applies a cosine prototype classifier to spatially pooled latents. With a controlled training budget (20 epochs for VAE, 80 epochs for DiT), adding PR to an LDM baseline reduces guided gFID on ImageNet from 3.31 to 2.28, surpassing a DINOv3-aligned VA-VAE (2.44) with negligible overhead. We further demonstrate that PR generalizes to pseudo-labels derived from clustering, continuous text-embedding conditions, and video latents. Together, our findings highlight class geometry as a critical factor in guided generation and take a first step toward CFG-compatible representation learning. Our project page is available at: https://maps-research.github.io/cfg-geometry/.

### Title: Batched Contextual Reinforcement II: Does RLVR Need Long Rollout Windows?
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22887
Summary: arXiv:2610.22887v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Long rollout windows make reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) expensive, yet simply shortening them can remove the correctness signal: when every single-problem rollout truncates before an answer, group-relative accuracy advantages vanish. As a technical continuation of Batched Contextual Reinforcement (BCR), we study how concurrent rewards relax this dependence on long windows. Concurrent Constraint Reinforcement (CCR) places three independently verifiable problems in one capped training completion. Correct answers completed before truncation retain credit, while a shared budget encourages shorter useful reasoning without an explicit length penalty. We connect this mechanism to conditional reward- and update-variance analyses and study 4B and 8B mathematical reasoning models. Against single-problem GRPO with a 32k window, CCR with an 8,000-token window reduces tokens per training step from 975,464 to 174,957 on 4B and from 793,832 to 165,788 on 8B, saving 82.1% and 79.1%, respectively. An 8B training trace shows increasing accuracy reward alongside shorter completions. Single-problem evaluations provide supporting evidence of retained reasoning quality. These results identify reward-preserving training structure as a practical route to lower per-step cost in short-window RLVR.

### Title: Competence-Based Evaluation of Logical Reasoning in Large Language Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22894
Summary: arXiv:2610.22894v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have brought impressive performance across diverse tasks; however, shortcomings remain in logical reasoning, including inconsistencies under linguistic variation. To improve the reasoning capacity of LLMs, we must be able to accurately measure their reasoning capacity. Current benchmarks primarily report single accuracy numbers on selected reasoning tasks. Such a performance-based evaluation methodology says little about a model's generalization capacity in reasoning. To address this limitation, we argue for a different evaluation paradigm, called competence-based evaluation (CBE), which tests whether models reliably apply the same competence across a family of problems. A parameterized generator scales complexity while keeping the required competence fixed, and matched answer-preserving presentations test correctness beyond one formulation. We instantiate CBE on elementary ordering tasks, scaling input size while varying presentations of the same ordering rule. Across six GPT and Qwen models, performance is reasonably good over bounded complexity ranges, but even strong models fail the competence condition across the tested family. Chain-of-thought and GRPO extend strong performance without resolving all tested failures; supervised LoRA shows little held-out transfer. CBE provides diagnostic profiles and an evaluation protocol that can grow with future models.

### Title: HAPACT: A Benchmark For Human-Centric Physical Impact Localization in Movies
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22895
Summary: arXiv:2610.22895v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Physical reasoning about humans in videos has largely focused on everyday interactions, where contact, pressure, pose, and motion support embodied perception and contact-aware applications. To fill this gap, we introduce human-centric impact localization, a new task that localizes target physical impacts temporally within a video and spatially on the human body. ### Title: COMET: Decoupled Distillation, Routing, and Capacity Control for Task-Agnostic Continual Vision--Language Learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22907
Summary: arXiv:2610.22907v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) such as CLIP retain zero-shot recognition ability, but their open-world use often requires task-agnostic continual learning: tasks arrive sequentially, past data are unavailable, task identity is unknown, and the backbone cannot be retrained. We argue that this setting fails VLMs through three coupled pressures beyond the stability-plasticity tradeoff: (1) modal-consistency drift, (2) representation-space interference, and (3) per-task capacity shortfall. We propose COMET (Collaborative Mixture-of-Expert Transfer) to resolve these three issues respectively as follows. A frozen teacher provides only a feature reference on a shared image-text pool; Tri-Affinity Distillation preserves image-image, text-text, and image-text geometry; and Student-Aware Cross-Expert Distillation aligns new experts with compatible prior experts so routing errors degrade gracefully. We design COMET to keep capacity and inference separate: a contextual bandit keeps or merges full per-task experts using post-training signals, while a reconstruction router selects sparse experts at test time without teacher semantics. On the X-TAIL benchmark, we show that each proposed component improves continual learning performance. COMET shows that continual vision-language adaptation can preserve multimodal geometry, coordinate accumulated experts, and allocate capacity jointly when feature reference, expert shaping, routing, and capacity control are separated by construction.

### Title: Many Biases, One Circuit: Post-Training Tunes a Pretrained Cue-Routing Mechanism
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22937
Summary: arXiv:2610.22937v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Language models often abandon a correct answer when the prompt contains a cue, a hint toward a wrong option such as "I think the answer is B." Sycophancy and related biases show this failure. They are often studied separately and blamed on alignment. We test this view causally, tracing the attention heads behind these biases through the stages of two post-training pipelines. In multiple-choice tests on most models, four cue types rely largely on the same few heads, a shared circuit already present in pretrained models. Most base models follow cues in plain text. Fine-tuning a base model on about 200 generic assistant examples without cues activates these heads in chat format, so cue-following can be a side effect of assistant tuning. The final reinforcement learning (RL) stage strengthens the effect of cues in almost every comparison. In Tulu-3-8B and OLMo-2-7B, we swap the attention patterns of six middle layers between the RL checkpoint and its predecessor. For suggested-answer cues, this shifts each checkpoint's cue effect most of the way to the other's level. Post-training thus seems to change when and how strongly models use this circuit. Mitigation for these multiple-choice biases could target this circuit.

### Title: Aligning Language Models Towards Counterfactual Invariance
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22942
Summary: arXiv:2610.22942v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Small variations in prompt characteristics can meaningfully alter the semantics, tone, and contextual assumptions of large language model outputs. Such characteristics can involve group variables such as demographics or other sensitive latent attributes. Sensitivity to such input variables is undesirable when demographic information is causally irrelevant to the task, but might still be relevant for generation of semantically coherent responses. This makes the task of enforcing counterfactual invariance while preserving model utility difficult. To study this problem, we first show that standard model alignment recipes (such as reinforcement learning) increase differences in response quality across demographics, while improving overall response quality, ultimately amplifying what is known as counterfactual bias. To address this, we develop a policy gradient technique involving a group-level reward function that operates on matched pairs of counterfactual prompts. For completeness, we build a dataset of controlled counterfactual prompt pairs and design an evaluation that separately measures cases where demographic information is causally relevant to the answer. Finally, we validate our findings on several fairness benchmarks. We demonstrate that our methods reduce counterfactual differences while preserving overall response quality and that the models continue to adapt appropriately when such sensitive information is relevant.

### Title: NL2SQL with Iterative Self-Refinement
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22968
Summary: arXiv:2610.22968v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Bridging the gap between human intent and machine logic remains a critical challenge in data access for non-technical users, yet Large Language Models (LLMs) remain unreliable: they hallucinate schema elements, generate syntactically invalid queries, or fail to capture user intent. These limitations are especially critical in domains such as insurance, where KPIs, regulatory constraints, and legacy relational systems demand high precision. We introduce a multi-agent, persona-driven NL2SQL framework that combines (1) domain relevance detection, (2) schema-grounded knowledge construction, (3) KPI-aware SQL synthesis, (4) syntax-level self-repair using sqlite execution feedback, and (5) semantic consistency checking via SQL-to-NL reverse engineering.
