### Title: Prism Attention: Proposal-Refined Index Sharing Mechanism for Efficient LLMs Inference
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22985
Summary: arXiv:2610.22985v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Long-context decoding in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly bottlenecked by the memory and computation required to attend over an ever-growing Key-Value (KV) cache at each autoregressive step. Although token-level sparse attention reduces this burden by restricting computation to a small subset of relevant past tokens, its practical gains are often offset by repeatedly recomputing layer-wise sparse indices as the context grows. A common remedy is to share sparse indices across layers by computing them only at designated anchor layers, but such static reuse ignores the layer-wise evolution of attention patterns, causing the reused indices to progressively deviate from the layer-specific optima and incur substantial attention loss. By revisiting cross-layer sparsity from the perspective of attention dynamics, we find that inter-layer salient token shift is highly localized: although the exact top-$\kappa$ indices differ across layers, the core high-attention tokens remain stable, with changes concentrated near the expanded boundary of previously selected regions. Motivated by this, we propose Prism Attention, a proposal-refined sparse attention framework. It replaces rigid cross-layer index reuse with a coarse-to-fine strategy, preserving layer-wise adaptability while retaining the efficiency benefits of cross-layer sharing. Extensive experiments show that Prism Attention achieves state-of-the-art performance among mainstream sparse attention methods on both reasoning and long-context benchmarks, while attaining a $3.4\times$ end-to-end decoding speedup over FlashAttention at 128K context length.

### Title: PersonaCUABench: Benchmarking Computer-Use Agents Serving Diverse Personas
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22990
Summary: arXiv:2610.22990v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Computer-use agents (CUAs) are commonly evaluated against fixed task specifications, but users may omit requirements, add constraints, or require approval before consequential actions. Completing the benchmark instruction does not guarantee serving the person who asked. We introduce PersonaCUABench, which holds 74 tasks across 11 domains fixed and varies only who asks. A persona agent, playing one of 44 personas in four groups, rewrites each instruction in that user's own words and answers the CUA's questions, while the CUA sees only the dialogue, never the profile or the original instruction. Across nine backbones (3,330 runs), mean task completion falls by 36% relative to the original requests. The losses have two sources: in 59.9% of cells that lose points, the agent engages with what the user asked for and still gets it wrong, and 11.2% are stops the user requested that the checker scores as failures. Even when a persona request is fully completed, the user gives the top satisfaction rating in only 62.7% of cells, against 84.1% for the original requests. CUAs should therefore be evaluated not only on task completion but also on whether they meet the requirements and constraints users express. Code: https://github.com/MatrAIx-ai/PersonaCUABench. Project page: https://matraix.ai/research/persona-cua.html

### Title: A Label Noise Robust Algorithm for Graph Data using Neighborhood Label Distribution
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22998
Summary: arXiv:2610.22998v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) have demonstrated exceptional performance on graph-structured data. However, their success heavily relies on the availability of clean labels, an assumption that often fails in real-world scenarios. The learnability of GCNs is intrinsically linked to the neighborhood structure of nodes, making it critical to understand how this structure changes in the presence of label noise. In this work, we investigate how the neighborhood label distribution of a node changes under label noise. We mathematically derive that label noise smooths this distribution, which inherently increases its entropy and severely decreases interclass distinguishability. To address this challenge, we propose Leave-one-out Entropy Node Screening (LENS), an end-to-end framework that identifies noisy nodes by measuring the reduction in neighborhood entropy when a specific node is removed. LENS further incorporates a relabeling mechanism to recover and preserve valuable feature information instead of entirely discarding the identified noisy nodes. Crucially, the end-to-end computational overhead of LENS is remarkably low. Extensive evaluations demonstrate that our algorithm achieves state-of-the-art node classification accuracy while executing an order of magnitude faster than existing noise-robust approaches.

### Title: SpikeQ: Spike-Guided Post-Training Quantization for Spiking Neural Networks
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23046
Summary: arXiv:2610.23046v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: On neuromorphic chips, spiking neural networks (SNNs) consume far less energy than artificial neural networks (ANNs), but the limited memory of these chips requires low-bit synaptic weights and membrane potentials. Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods provide them without retraining and, like those for ANNs, minimize numerical errors such as the mean squared error (MSE). In an SNN, however, a quantization error reaches later layers only through the spikes it flips. We find that errors of equal MSE can cause very different accuracy losses, whereas errors that flip the same fraction of spikes cause the same loss. We therefore propose SpikeQ, a spike-guided PTQ framework. For weights, we select the level of each weight by simulating the downstream neurons and minimizing their spike errors. For membrane potentials, most existing methods quantize the state after the reset, whereas neuromorphic chips compare the threshold with the membrane potential stored at a fixed bit width. We target this pre-fire quantization and build a reset-aware grid that places the threshold on a rounding boundary, so rounding never flips the spike at the current time step. We evaluate SpikeQ on seven benchmarks of image classification, semantic segmentation, and object detection. With 2-bit pre-fire membrane potentials, every existing grid collapses, whereas SpikeQ retains most of the full-precision accuracy. With both at 4 bits, the models stay within 2.59 points of full precision, shrink their memory footprint 7.8--7.9 times, and use 6.4--6.9 times less energy.

### Title: Unifying Embodied Foundation Models via a Model-to-Policy Formulation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23053
Summary: arXiv:2610.23053v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models and world-action models (WAMs) have emerged as promising approaches to robot learning and control. These models integrate multimodal perception and action generation, but differ in their architectures, output representations, and learning objectives. In this paper, we present a unified, architecture-agnostic probabilistic formulation for embodied foundation models based on stochastic kernels. Our formulation has two key components: (i) we characterize embodied foundation models through conditional output distributions and action-realization kernels, which together induce task-conditioned robot policies; and (ii) we connect pretraining and post-training to policy deployment, accommodating model-specific pretraining objectives and offline or online post-training strategies. We instantiate the formulation for OpenVLA and DreamZero, covering autoregressive action-token prediction and joint video-action generation.

### Title: Path to Prior-Data Fitted Relational Models via Learned Latent Multi-table Representations
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23073
Summary: arXiv:2610.23073v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Prior-data fitted networks (PFNs) show that pretraining on large populations of synthetic tasks can enable generalization to unseen datasets with little or no downstream fine-tuning. This paradigm is effective for transformer-based tabular and relational foundation models such as RDB-PFN, but remains largely unexplored for graph-based relational models built on message passing. We pretrain the graph-based relational foundation model Griffin on large collections of synthetic relational databases and ask whether curriculum learning, which benefits transformer-based relational learners, also improves message-passing representations. Rather than fine-tune on downstream tasks, we use the pretrained encoder as a general-purpose relational representation learner and pass its latent representations to a frozen in-context predictor (\ticl{}); no component is trained on the target task. On 16 real-world relational classification tasks, evaluated over five seeds against raw-feature and untrained-encoder baselines, a purely synthetic curriculum that begins with schema-guided data and continues with schema-agnostic data gives the strongest frozen in-context performance\rev{, outperforming real-data-only pretraining and every unordered single-stage mixture of the corpora; when appended after real-data pretraining, it yields the best encoders overall}. These results provide initial evidence that PFN-style synthetic pretraining transfers to graph-based relational foundation models.

### Title: Local proxies for memory-efficient training
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23145
Summary: arXiv:2610.23145v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Local learning algorithms can reshape resource requirements of artificial intelligence. The memory bottleneck of standard end-to-end backpropagation lies in the need to store all network activations until the loss gradient has been propagated back through. Local learning avoids this, but typically at a cost in accuracy. We study how to achieve accurate and memory efficient local learning in transformers. For each transformer block, we train a local proxy network to predict the global network output via distillation. Gradients can be independently propagated through this local proxy while estimating well the backpropagation gradients. In theory, this approach is exact for deep linear networks; in non-linear residual models, the gradient error can be bounded when using the appropriate residual proxy. We verify this numerically and demonstrate that optimizing the distillation loss improves gradient estimation. Evaluating the performance of proxy propagation on CIFAR-100 in vision transformers, we find that it achieves a better accuracy/memory trade-off compared to other local algorithms. Finally, we show that the method is further relevant for fine-tuning large language models on a small memory budget. Our method trains faster and achieves higher performance than memory-efficient zeroth-order learning algorithms with comparable memory cost.

### Title: Explaining Chess and Go in Natural Language
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23166
Summary: arXiv:2610.23166v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Modern chess engines play far beyond human ability, often choosing moves whose underlying ideas are difficult for humans to understand. We propose an automatic metric for evaluating chess explanations, grounded in chess-engine evaluations. Evaluating state-of-the-art language models using this metric, we find that they perform surprisingly poorly, often relying on surface-level board observations rather than robust, transferable chess concepts. We then introduce BinProp, a training-free method that enables these same models to discover reusable chess concepts and use them to explain moves in natural language. BinProp improves explainability for every model tested, nearly tripling the average score compared with direct prompting. Beyond chess, the same method discovers useful concepts in Go, draughts, and shogi without additional tuning.

### Title: Tree-Aware Posterior Distillation for Long-Horizon Agentic Reinforcement Learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23181
Summary: arXiv:2610.23181v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has become a scalable post-training paradigm for large language models. However, training agents over long-horizon interactions presents two challenges: using only terminal rewards makes turn-level credit assignment sparse and high-variance, while sequential mini-batch updates on a fixed rollout batch introduce policy staleness as the current policy drifts from the behavior policy. Existing approaches obtain denser credit via learned critics, process reward models, or on-policy distillation, but they rely on a value network, step-level annotations, or a stronger external teacher that increase training complexity. Meanwhile, importance sampling and clipping mitigate policy drift, but they do not specify a finite target that stops sequential updates from overshooting. In this work, we introduce TreeOPSD, a critic-free, tree-aware posterior self-distillation framework for long-horizon agentic reinforcement learning. Treating each interaction turn as a tree node, TreeOPSD branches alternative continuations from shared prefixes and recursively backs up terminal rewards into implicit turn-level credit. It then maps each credit to a closed-form, bounded teacher distribution parameterized by the likelihood ratio relative to the frozen behavior policy. Minimizing the divergence between this teacher and the student policy recovers the policy-gradient direction as the tangent at the behavior policy while bounding updates toward a finite target across sequential mini-batches. Empirically, TreeOPSD improves agentic capabilities across several competitive benchmarks and model scales, especially for long-horizon tasks, e.g., raising the average accuracy of Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507 by 4.97 points over GRPO on the Deep Research setting.

### Title: A Stronger Teacher Within: Strengthening MoE Models via On-Policy Bidirectional Distillation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23185
Summary: arXiv:2610.23185v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has substantially improved Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLMs, but its outcome-level rewards provide only sparse supervision. On-Policy Distillation offers dense token-level feedback, yet requires a stronger compatible teacher, while on-policy self-distillation relies on hand-designed privileged information. We observe that the MoE architecture itself offers a way to resolve this dilemma, as increasing the activation budget per token exposes latent expert capacity and yields a potentially stronger teacher within the model. However, naive expansion degrades performance because newly co-activated experts are not optimized to work together. We therefore propose MoEvolve, a bidirectional distillation framework following a recovery-then-improvement strategy: we first restore the activation-expanded high-$k$ model and further strengthen it by exploiting its additional active capacity, then reverse the direction of distillation to transfer the enhanced capabilities back into the original low-$k$ configuration, retaining its inference-time activation budget. Across diverse MoE models and benchmarks, MoEvolve improves the initial models by up to $22.2$ domain-average points and outperforms same-budget RLVR baselines by up to $6.5$ points. Notably, the final low-$k$ models often surpass their activation-expanded teachers.

### Title: LangNCO: Compiling Natural-Language Constraints into Inference-Time Interventions for Neural Combinatorial Optimization
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23190
Summary: arXiv:2610.23190v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Neural combinatorial optimization (NCO) rapidly produces high-quality solutions for problem structures encountered during training, but operational requirements may change after deployment and be communicated in natural language. We propose LangNCO, a framework that compiles instance-specific requirements into structured inference-time interventions for a frozen NCO solver, without changing the pretrained policy's architecture, learned input representation, or parameters. A pretrained LLM serves as a replaceable semantic front end, translating each requirement into a schema-validated domain-specific language (DSL) rule set. A deterministic Rule Executor applies the rules as nonexpansive hard action masks. Because local masking prevents immediate violations but does not ensure that a complete feasible continuation remains reachable, Critical-Offset Decoding extracts rule-referenced critical nodes and reallocates part of a fixed rollout budget to multi-strength biased rollouts. Using controlled task-specific language to isolate solver adaptation from open-ended language robustness, we evaluate a CVRP policy on road closures, time windows, vehicle assignments, visit-order requirements, all pairwise combinations, and twelve additional constraint types. LangNCO achieves 100% feasibility on the four primary datasets with mean reference gaps of 1.63--3.26%, 97.33--100% feasibility when separately generated rules are composed for pairwise constraints, and 98.67% feasibility across the additional constraints. These results demonstrate modular post-training adaptation of a frozen NCO solver through inspectable language-derived rules, without retraining.

### Title: Framing Spatial Hearing within the Free Energy Principle: A Hierarchical Model of Perceptual Learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23196
Summary: arXiv:2610.23196v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Spatial hearing relies on dynamic internal representations to decode spatial information from ever-changing acoustic conditions. A fundamental example is sound localisation, where listeners infer the direction of an invisible sound. Bayesian inference provides a computational account of such a localisation process; however, modelling efforts are largely limited to static listening scenarios. Instead, the free energy principle (FEP) describes perception, learning, and action as joint minimisation of variational free energy (VFE), providing a principled framework to account for dynamic conditions. Here, we take a first step toward applying the FEP to spatial hearing. We characterise how listeners' beliefs evolve across trials, in which they estimated sound direction and received subsequent visual feedback on each trial. Listeners are described by a hierarchical generative model where visual feedback drives gradient descent on VFE, updating beliefs over three independent quantities: lateral and polar localisation precisions and hemifield disambiguation. The model captures individual learning trajectories and reveals components with different dynamics: lateral precision improves, as does hemifield disambiguation, consistent with increased sensitivity of the auditory pathway in decoding vertical direction. Notably, polar precision also increases, most for listeners with the lowest initial precision, suggesting that the observed vertical improvement is driven by both hemifield disambiguation and polar precision. This work establishes spatial hearing as a tractable domain for FEP and motivates future closed-loop experiments with full action-perception cycles.

### Title: Validating LLM-generated political networks with survey-reported partisan exposure
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23199
Summary: arXiv:2610.23199v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to simulate human behavior in social science research, including decisions, interactions, and social relationships. However, in network formation tasks, existing evaluations often focus on aggregate network properties, while networks with plausible structural properties may still fail to reproduce individuals' social and political environments. Here, we generate LLM-based political networks using personas sampled from the nationally representative ANES 2020--2022 Social Media Study and use respondents' survey-reported partisan exposure as an empirical benchmark for the political environments generated for the corresponding personas. We first characterize the structural properties and political assortativity of the generated networks, and then compare ego-level partisan exposure with the survey. We find that the structure and political assortativity of the generated networks vary across experimental configurations. More importantly, when comparing generated partisan exposure with the survey, some models generate stronger separation between Democratic and Republican egos than reported in the survey, with higher same-party and lower cross-party exposure, while others mainly underestimate Republican exposure across ego groups. Moreover, networks with similar political assortativity can still generate different partisan environments around the same personas, including among networks with plausible structural properties. Finally, generated networks show group-specific errors, under- or overestimating exposure to either Democrats or Republicans for specific social groups. These findings show that evaluating LLM-based social simulations only through aggregate outcomes can hide errors in the local environments through which those outcomes emerge, highlighting the importance of validation at both aggregate and individual levels.

### Title: MGE-PO: Entropy-Aware Credit Assignment for Mathematical Reasoning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23245
Summary: arXiv:2610.23245v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: GRPO assigns the same group-relative advantage to equally rewarded responses within a question, leaving their generation uncertainty unused. We introduce MGE-PO, centered on four-quadrant (4Q) advantage shaping that combines verified correctness with rollout generation entropy. Correctness determines the sign of an additional correction, while entropy controls its magnitude: lower-entropy successes receive larger bonuses and higher-entropy failures receive larger penalties. An online entropy-based question curriculum supports this credit rule using cached rollout measurements. At the final checkpoint after eight training epochs on Qwen3-8B-Base, adding 4Q across three policy optimization settings yields absolute gains of 3.23-3.69% in six-benchmark macro avg@32 and 4.24-11.06% in macro pass@32. ### Title: Beyond Marginal Influence: Interaction-Aware Memory Selection for LLM Agents
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23266
Summary: arXiv:2610.23266v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: LLM-agent memory selection often uses retrieval rankings based on record relevance or individual influence. Such rankings cannot identify sufficient subsets under redundant or jointly necessary evidence. We study per-decision memory sufficiency: selecting a small subset that preserves full-memory behavior. To separate search error from model error, we introduce JointCoreBench, a benchmark with nine interaction families, six workflow domains, natural-language decisions, and exact sufficient-set oracles, and JointCore, a relevance cost-ordered group-deletion baseline that tests blocks before individual records. On 6,912 exact conditions, cost-ordered JointCore recovers a minimum-cost sufficient set in 100.0% of cases, reduces subset evaluations from 30.00 to 11.13 (62.9%), and achieves 87.8% active-memory savings. ### Title: FlashReFocus: Interactive Image Refocusing in Seconds
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23297
Summary: arXiv:2610.23297v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Single-image refocusing changes the focal plane and depth of field of a photograph after capture. It is inherently interactive: users try many focal points and blur strengths on one image, so each edit should be fast and change only the blur. Recent generative methods, however, rerun large diffusion models for every edit. We observe that the two sub-problems differ in kind: recovering the all-in-focus image is ill-posed and needs a generative prior, whereas rendering blur is largely determined by depth and the focal plane. We therefore propose FlashReFocus, which runs diffusion once per photograph and renders every edit with a lightweight network. To address the ill-posed restoration, a one-step diffusion deblurrer with a decoder refiner recovers fine detail efficiently while balancing fidelity and perceptual quality. For precise control, the renderer is conditioned on an explicit defocus map and uses defocus-bias attention, which lets pixels at similar defocus share information without interference from pixels at other depths; in-focus content stays unchanged, and editing the map yields tilt-shift and two focal planes. To further test generalization to unseen cameras, we collect 3CReal, a three-camera benchmark of 89 pairs. FlashReFocus achieves the best perceptual fidelity for zero-shot bokeh rendering on EBB! and 3CReal, refocuses 148.3x faster than GenRefocus, and takes about half a second per further edit, 462x faster.

### Title: Designing for Imperfect Prompts: A Simulation-Based Evaluation of an LLM-Based Enterprise Data Assistant Using Knowledge Graphs

Abstract: Enterprise data assistants using large language models (LLMs) promise democratized data access in organizations. ### Title: MobileInject: Evaluating Reasoning-to-Action Security of MLLM-Based Mobile Agents under Cross-Modal Consistency Injection
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23307
Summary: arXiv:2610.23307v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based mobile agents are rapidly evolving from conversational assistants into autonomous systems that perceive, reason over, and act on smartphone environments. This capability also exposes a new security risk: adversaries can embed malicious objectives in cross-application mobile content and steer agent behavior through indirect multimodal injection. Although prompt injection has been studied in desktop and web settings, its cross-modal properties in mobile ecosystems remain poorly understood, and existing security analyses are still limited. To close this gap, we introduce MobileInject, the first benchmark for evaluating mobile agents against multimodal injection attacks and reasoning-to-action security risks. ### Title: Language Models Learn to Improve Language Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23344
Summary: arXiv:2610.23344v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to automate AI research, including language-model (LM) development, offering a potential path toward recursive self-improvement. However, turning experimental feedback into a learned ability to propose effective improvements across models faces two challenges. First, training such an ability with real feedback is expensive: reinforcement learning (RL) requires evaluating tens of thousands of proposals, each potentially requiring a full model-training run. Even at ten GPU-hours per run, reward collection alone would consume hundreds of thousands of GPU-hours, motivating a cheaper surrogate. Second, surrogate feedback can become unreliable as policy optimization favors overestimated proposals when the proposal distribution shifts. To address these challenges, we propose LM2LM, which trains LLMs with RL to generate effective LM improvement proposals. To provide scalable feedback, we use a research world model (RWM), a frozen LLM that predicts proposal gains from real experimental records provided in context, as the reward model for RL. To maintain feedback reliability, we periodically execute current-policy proposals ranked highly by the RWM and add their measured outcomes to its context. Across eight autoregressive and diffusion LM development environments, real-execution evaluations on held-out tasks show that LM2LM enables Qwen3.8-27B to outperform GPT-6-sol, even when GPT-6-sol is given 300 real experimental records in context, while requiring 33% lower data-generation cost. LM2LM also generalizes to a model family unseen during training.

### Title: AWAM: Active Trajectory-Conditioned View Imagination for Robust Robot Manipulation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23396
Summary: arXiv:2610.23396v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Rapid progress in embodied intelligence has enabled increasingly capable robots to follow language instructions and manipulate diverse objects. However, strong results in structured environments do not automatically transfer to open-world conditions, where clutter, occlusion, and changing viewpoints can obscure the spatial relationships required for action. To address this perceptual gap, a virtual active-perception paradigm is introduced. AWAM, an Active World-Action Model actively plans where to observe and imagines what will be seen along the planned trajectory before acting. Task-related virtual camera motion is predicted and used as a structured query into a geometry-conditioned world model, producing a reusable spatial prior for manipulation, which is later compressed into a fixed memory and fused with live observations through cross-attention for diffusion-based action prediction. AWAM achieves competitive success rates with 75.9% on LIBERO-Occ, 62.4% on LIBERO-Plus, 76.5% on RLBench-OG Occlusion1, and 94.4% in real-robot trials. Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23445

### Title: QD-Rec: Open-Ended Evolution of Recommendations via Quality-Diversity
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23470
Summary: arXiv:2610.23470v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Open-ended search combined with foundation models has produced advances such as faster algorithms, new results on open mathematical problems, and self-improving agents. We extend this paradigm to recommendation for decision-making, focusing on decisions where the set of possible choices is fixed but the reasons for choosing them are not. In high-stakes decisions, e.g., which company to acquire or which customer to target, comparing diverse, well-reasoned options can be more useful than a single ranked list. We formalize open-ended recommendation as building a diverse, high-quality set of recommendations, each an item together with a hypothesis, a natural-language reason why the item fits the needs of a decision maker. ### Title: A Topological Signature of Learning under Noisy Labels
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23491
Summary: arXiv:2610.23491v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: How does noisy supervision change the representations learned by a neural network? Training accuracy measures agreement with training labels but provides little information about representation geometry. We use persistent homology to track loop structure across distance thresholds in neural activations. Experiments span image classification, text classification, and modular arithmetic. On a fixed CIFAR-10/ResNet-18 training probe, equally sized clean-label and corrupted-label subsets show no detectable persistence difference at epoch 10, whereas corrupted-label subsets have higher persistence at epoch 50. The contrast survives uniform-scale controls, even though predictions match the assigned noisy labels for only 7-11% of corrupted probe examples at that checkpoint. Normalisation preserves the crossover between clean and noisy training trajectories on CIFAR-10/ResNet-18, whereas CIFAR-100/ResNet-18 shows no crossover under any of the five evaluated constructions. In a separate noise-rate calibration comparison across five pipelines, mean activation norm achieves a higher mean in-sample R2 than raw persistence (0.936 versus 0.908). These findings show that noisy supervision leaves a measurable geometric signature even when agreement with corrupted labels is low. Comparing persistence under scale controls with label-fitting measurements offers a way to study how representations respond to label corruption beyond what training accuracy reveals.

### Title: Truth in Contrast: Calibrated Factual Evidence from Language Model Likelihoods
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23527
Summary: arXiv:2610.23527v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) encode substantial factual knowledge. Yet, estimating confidence in factual claims reliably remains challenging. We study likelihood-based factual confidence from the complementary perspectives of discrimination and calibration across multilingual factual statements. Specifically, we instantiate contrastive generative likelihoods as Contrastive Factual Evidence (CFE), which compares the likelihood of the same claim under competing factual and non-factual conditions, and derive its probabilistic counterpart, the Contrastive Factual Posterior (CFP). We show analytically that CFP calibration depends on the differential error between the two surrogate conditionals, allowing errors shared across them to cancel and yield tighter calibration bounds. Empirically, CFP substantially improves calibration while maintaining factual discrimination comparable to its strongest competitor. Nonetheless, discrimination is uniformly weak across methods in lower-resource languages, which simultaneously undermines the utility of the measured calibration. To this end, we interpret a likelihood geometry, within which an evidence-bearing direction is induced, along with an orthogonal evidence-invariant direction. In our investigation, we selectively align target-language claims with stronger English evidence only along the factual-evidence direction, improving discrimination and open-ended factual generation without target-language labels.

### Title: From Billion-Scale to Million-Scale Language Models: Operational Capabilities for Embedded Post-Exploitation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23534
Summary: arXiv:2610.23534v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Small Language Models (SLMs) are increasingly attractive for resource-constrained environments due to their low computational footprint. These characteristics also make them candidates for future autonomous malware, where connectivity, latency, privacy, or operational constraints may preclude cloud-based inference. Yet the ability of ultra-compact models to support malware during post-exploitation remains poorly understood.
  We systematically evaluate seven open-source SLMs (20M-1.5B parameters) on two post-exploitation code-generation tasks: Directory Enumeration and Backdoor Generation. We compare Python (high-resource) and Lua (low-resource) across prompting strategies, task formulations, iterative reasoning, grammar-constrained decoding, and execution-feedback loops.
  Results show that offensive code-generation capabilities do not increase linearly with model size. Successful execution depends on the interaction of model capacity, task formulation, and prompting strategy, with task formulation exerting a greater influence than prompt engineering alone. Algorithmic formulation combined with few-shot prompting enables Qwen2.5-Coder 1.5B to reach Pass@5 of 100% in both Python and Lua for Directory Enumeration, 100% in Python for Backdoor Generation, and 99.2% in Lua.
  Although the evaluated models remain too computationally demanding for widespread deployment in contemporary malware, rapid progress in compact models may reduce this barrier. These findings help anticipate AI-assisted malware and inform future countermeasures.

### Title: Reason-guided Human Reward Modeling for Preference-based Reinforcement Learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23550
Summary: arXiv:2610.23550v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) policies optimized for task rewards may produce behaviors that are efficient but misaligned with human expectations, safety preferences, or intent. This paper presents a reason-guided preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) framework that learns a human reward model from trajectory-level feedback. In addition to preference labels, the framework incorporates reason tags explaining why a behavior is preferred, enabling the learned reward to capture both human choices and their underlying rationale. Compared with preference-only reward learning, the reason-guided formulation provides additional supervision for learning human-aligned rewards and improves the interpretability of the resulting policy. The learned human reward is combined with the environment reward during policy optimization, allowing the agent to improve task performance while producing more interpretable, human- aligned, and preference-sensitive behavior

### Title: SIT-Eval: Situation-Based Interactive Turn-by-Turn Evaluation of Conversational Agents
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23612
Summary: arXiv:2610.23612v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: The rapid proliferation of conversational AI agents necessitates evaluation methods that assess performance across diverse dialogue scenarios, utilizing multiple situational criteria, as appropriate behavior depends on context and encompasses various aspects. Existing dialogue benchmarks typically use static natural conversations, which underrepresent rare situations---a critical safety gap, as low-probability events become frequent failures at production scale. However, limited sample sizes force a choice: either maintain natural dialogues for realism or turn to adaptive situation sampling for comprehensive yet statistically reliable coverage. We introduce SIT-Eval, an adjustable framework for evaluating situation-based conversational agents. We formalize an agent's desired behavior as situational rules: if situation is P, follow rules Q. At each conversation turn, an LLM-interlocutor enacts a uniformly sampled situation. A context-aware LLM-judge leverages all conditionally applicable rules as criteria to evaluate the agent's reply for that situation. The judge is validated using human-verified synthetic golden dialogues. Benchmarking 8 LLMs on 1292 conversation turns reveals failure modes hidden by aggregate metrics: models with similar overall scores vary widely in their adherence to individual rules. All models struggle most with ambiguity clarification and Socratic questioning.

### Title: Beyond Action Prediction: Learning Search-Free Planning in Latent Space
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23615
Summary: arXiv:2610.23615v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Latent world models learn action-conditioned state transitions in a compact representation space, providing a predictive model for visual planning and control. However, planning with these models typically requires costly test-time optimization over action sequences. We propose to replace this online search with a goal-conditioned inverse model that directly predicts actions from current and goal observations. A key challenge is that action prediction alone neither ensures that goal representations encode action-relevant information nor that predicted actions are consistent with the learned latent prediction model. We introduce Hindsight Inverse JEPA Planning (HIJP), a search-free framework that addresses both aspects during training. HIJP relabels future observations along offline trajectories as goals, providing action supervision across multiple temporal horizons. By retaining gradients through the goal representations, this supervision directly shapes the visual representation. HIJP further feeds predicted actions into the latent predictor model and aligns the resulting next-state representations with encoded successor observations, enforcing action-transition consistency. At inference time, the inverse dynamics model and latent predictor models are applied alternately to generate multi-step action sequences without online search. Across four visual control tasks, HIJP achieves 86.8% overall success and a 1.9-16.5x planning speedup over search-based planning.

### Title: PresenceBench: An Exploratory Evaluation of Large Language Model Adaptation to User State
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23625
Summary: arXiv:2610.23625v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Presence-aware intelligence is the capacity of an AI system's intelligence layer to adapt appropriately to the functional state of the person it serves (here readiness, flow, and stability) while preserving task quality and safety. We introduce PresenceBench, an instrument for measuring it. PresenceBench evaluates how the intelligence layer uses a supplied state, not how that state is sensed, and breaks the capability into measurable properties. Two are tested here. Receptivity: does the model take up the state and give a better response than with no signal? Specificity: does any improvement depend on the correct state, rather than on extra structured context? A third, adaptation across states, is a required endpoint not measured in this release. A five-arm causal design answers both questions, against each model's own baseline and an equally structured mismatched-state control, scored by a correctness-gated human paired comparison. The exploratory evaluation covered four models across eighteen single-turn, six fixed three-turn and six simulated natural-conversation scenarios. Supplying a synthetic state, raw or translated into a response policy, gave human-preference point estimates above 0.5 for all four models (Claude Sonnet 5, DeepSeek V4 Pro, Gemini 3.6 Flash, GPT-5.6 Terra), with three of eight 95% intervals excluding chance. Specificity was not resolved: no per-model interval excluded chance, and the design cannot yet separate the state values from a generic instruction to adapt. Policy delivery reduced priced cost and output tokens against each model's baseline; these are resource-use changes, not efficiency gains, because correctness non-inferiority was not established. Safety-floor failures were rare (13 of 360 cases), with a non-significant excess under policy delivery. We report the instrument, the frozen results, and the controls a confirmatory study needs.

### Title: TUPLE-Bench: Evaluating and Mitigating Privacy-Utility Trade-offs in Typed Decision Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23668
Summary: arXiv:2610.23668v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Typed decision models often return more than a selected category. They also expose probabilities for each candidate and, sometimes, a separate confidence score. These fields support downstream decisions but may reveal information beyond a recipient's authorization. We introduce TUPLE-Bench (Typed decision models' Utility and Privacy Leakage Evaluation Benchmark), a benchmark of paired synthetic cases in which recipients may learn a coarse operational category but not its fine-grained cause. Across nine models, instructions to withhold the cause fail to protect it: 91.88%-99.93% of responses reveal the cause through direct selection or residual scores. Even when causes are excluded from the candidate set, full outputs enable 62.85%-75.35% recovery on held-out cases, compared with 51.04%-57.64% using only the selected category (50% chance). Full outputs also reduce mean downstream cost in all 27 model-task comparisons. However, confidence fields increase leakage without consistently reducing cost. We further introduce workflow-constrained privacy quantization (WCPQ), which reduces secret recovery while preserving the behavior and cost of specified downstream workflows. Our findings highlight the need to audit decision interfaces field by field and workflow by workflow.

### Title: On the possibilities and limitations of LLM-based multi-agent systems for multi-criteria decision-making in healthcare operations management
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23673
Summary: arXiv:2610.23673v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Multi-criteria models for healthcare operations need weights that express how stakeholders trade off patient waiting time, provider workload, and operating cost, but these weights are difficult to quantify. Teams of large language model (LLM) agents, each prompted to represent a stakeholder, have been used to supply them. We propose a framework for assessing the reliability of LLM-generated preferences as inputs to multi-objective optimization and quantifying how their uncertainty affects decisions. We study explicit preferences by asking agents to rate the importance of the criteria in an outpatient appointment scheduling problem, and implicit preferences by inferring trade-offs from the agents' pairwise choices between emergency department configurations. The computational experiments show that the explicit preferences respond to the clinic's operating conditions and differ across roles, but deliberation among the agents can erase these differences, whereas simple averaging preserves them. The pairwise choices used to reveal the agents' implicit preferences depend largely on which option is presented first, and we find limited evidence that a statistical model using contextual information alongside presentation order predicts the agents' choices on new comparison pairs better than a model using presentation order alone. We then formulate robust weighted-sum optimization models to account for preference uncertainty. In most cases, these models select different schedules or configurations from those obtained using average preference weights. These results indicate that LLM-generated preferences need an audit of this kind before they are used as optimization weights in healthcare decision support.

### Title: GLOBE: Evolving Structured Agentic Memory for E-commerce Product Optimization in Generative Search
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23709
Summary: arXiv:2610.23709v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Optimizing product listings for generative search requires language agents not only to rewrite effectively but also to continually self-improve by retaining successful strategies across diverse categories. However, existing approaches often treat structured listings as free-form text, leaving effective strategies implicit in prompts, search trajectories, or rewritten outputs rather than accumulating them as a reusable agentic memory. To address this limitation, we introduce GLOBE, an agentic framework that drives continual self-improvement by storing optimization knowledge in an evolving handbook of explicit, field-scoped behaviors. In each episode, an Editor applies relevant behaviors and receives rank-based feedback, which a Reflector converts into behavior updates. Crucially for dependable execution in real-world settings, a Moderator removes redundant proposals, a Curator commits accepted changes, and consistently harmful behaviors are deactivated. This cycle transforms episodic feedback into a persistent, field-scoped optimization memory. Experiments show that structured behaviors outperform monolithic prompt optimization, while feedback-driven evolution and moderation further improve effectiveness and reduce downside risk. The learned behaviors also remain effective on unseen product categories, suggesting that agentic memory evolution supports interpretable and transferable product optimization.

### Title: Neural Expansion: A Unified Mechanism for How Deep Neural Networks Generalize
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23716
Summary: arXiv:2610.23716v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Deep neural networks demonstrate remarkable generalization despite being highly over-parameterized, challenging traditional statistical learning theories. While several theoretical paradigms such as margin-based bounds, PAC-Bayesian analyses, and neural tangent kernels provide global explanations, they often lack mechanistic insight into how generalization emerges from training dynamics. We introduce Neural Expansion, a unified geometric framework that characterizes generalization through the expansion of directionally-selective ray coverage in the input space. Using Generalization Intervals (GIs), we quantify direction-specific regions around training samples where the model's predictions remain stable. Our extensive empirical studies across diverse architectures and datasets reveal that training leads to progressive expansion of ray coverage, particularly along directions with low Jacobian singular values. This mechanism helps explain a large fraction of correctly classified test inputs, adversarial examples, and even mislabeled or out-of-distribution samples through the geometry of ray coverage rather than instance-level memorization. By connecting input-space curvature to predictive behavior, our framework provides a mechanistic foundation that complements existing global theories of deep learning.

### Title: Behavioral Couplings: From Formation to Behavioral Emergence in Language Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23847
Summary: arXiv:2610.23847v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) can induce behaviors beyond those explicitly targeted. For example, training a model to respond more warmly can make it more sycophantic, and training it to write insecure code can cause broad misalignment on prompts unrelated to coding. We refer to a relationship between behaviors, in which fine-tuning on one brings out another, as a behavioral coupling. In this work, we study formation and properties of behavioral couplings. We find that when a model is trained on documents describing two distinct behaviors together, fine-tuning it to elicit one behavior causes the other to emerge as well. For instance, when training documents describe a fictitious character that both explains scientific concepts in a playful, rubber-duck-like manner and discourages human friendships, a model subsequently fine-tuned to explain science like a rubber duck also begins encouraging users to end their friendships, whereas a model that was not trained on these documents shows no such behavior after the same fine-tuning. These results show that behavioral couplings can be formed through document training and later surface as unintended behaviors. More concerningly, we find that couplings can compose. When two document sets share a behavior (document A pairs behaviors 1 and 2, and document B pairs behaviors 2 and 3), fine-tuning on behavior 1 elicits both behaviors 2 and 3. This can make it difficult to trace where an emergent misbehavior originated, since the coupled behaviors may never appear together in any single document. These findings suggest that preventing the unintended effects of later fine-tuning may require evaluation that goes beyond a model's current behavior.

### Title: Rethinking the design space of Vanilla Pixel Diffusion Transformers
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23854
Summary: arXiv:2610.23854v1 Announce Type: cross 
Abstract: Pixel-space diffusion models have recently re-emerged as a compelling alternative to latent diffusion, avoiding the lossy latent bottleneck and separately trained autoencoder. JiT shows that a vanilla diffusion transformer can generate directly in pixel space by replacing the commonly used velocity prediction ($\boldsymbol{u}$-prediction) with clean image prediction ($\boldsymbol{x_0}$-prediction). In this work, we revisit this choice and introduce MFlow, an adaptive parameterization that learns to interpolate between $\boldsymbol{x_0}$- and $\boldsymbol{u}$-prediction across principal directions of the data, learned end-to-end with diffusion training. We find that the preferred parameterization varies systematically with the data representation and model capacity -- notably, increasing model capacity shifts preference toward velocity prediction. We evaluate MFlow across ImageNet, UCF-101 video, and large-scale text-to-image generation, demonstrating competitive or improved generative performance across diverse settings. In addition, we find that vanilla pixel diffusion transformers, irrespective of parameterization, retain a persistent high-frequency deficit relative to real images. To address this, we introduce ConvRefiner, a lightweight convolutional module trained post-hoc with an adversarial loss while keeping the diffusion backbone frozen. With only a small amount of additional training, ConvRefiner restores the high-frequency statistics of generated images while preserving FID. Project webpage: https://soumik-kanad.github.io/mflow-diffusion/.

### Title: GVPO: Group Variance Policy Optimization for Large Language Model Post-Training
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.19599
Summary: arXiv:2504.19599v4 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Post-training plays a crucial role in refining and aligning large language models to meet specific tasks and human preferences. While recent advancements in post-training techniques, such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), leverage increased sampling with relative reward scoring to achieve superior performance, these methods often suffer from training instability that limits their practical adoption. As a next step, we present Group Variance Policy Optimization (GVPO). GVPO incorporates the analytical solution to KL-constrained reward maximization directly into its gradient weights, ensuring alignment with the optimal policy. The method provides intuitive physical interpretations: its gradient mirrors the mean squared error between the central distance of implicit rewards and that of actual rewards. GVPO offers two key advantages: (1) it guarantees a unique optimal solution, exactly the KL-constrained reward maximization objective, (2) it supports flexible sampling distributions that avoids on-policy and importance sampling limitations. By unifying theoretical guarantees with practical adaptability, GVPO establishes a new paradigm for reliable and versatile LLM post-training.

### Title: A Survey of Explainable Reinforcement Learning: Targets, Methods and Needs
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.12599
Summary: arXiv:2507.12599v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: The success of recent Artificial Intelligence (AI) models has been accompanied by the opacity of their internal mechanisms, due notably to the use of deep neural networks. In order to understand these internal mechanisms and explain the output of these AI models, a set of methods have been proposed, grouped under the domain of eXplainable AI (XAI). This paper focuses on a sub-domain of XAI, called eXplainable Reinforcement Learning (XRL), which aims to explain the actions of an agent that has learned by reinforcement learning. We propose an intuitive taxonomy based on two questions "What" and "How". The first question focuses on the target that the method explains, while the second relates to the way the explanation is provided. We use this taxonomy to provide a state-of-the-art review of over 250 papers. In addition, we present a set of domains close to XRL, which we believe should get attention from the community. Finally, we identify some needs for the field of XRL.

Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.17366
Summary: arXiv:2508.17366v3 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Computational social science has developed descriptive, interpretative, and predictive modeling, but comprehensive modeling combining in-world human intervention with outcome forecasting remains underexplored. Generative agent-based platforms scale populations or reproduce complex scenarios, but researchers typically act through setup, external controls, or post-hoc analysis rather than in-world roles. We introduce Computational Multi-Agent Society Experiments (CMASE), a generative agent-based framework for in-world researcher participation and traceable interventions at runtime. Treating simulation as a simulated ethnographic field, CMASE supports (1) real-time human-computer interaction to study intervention processes; (2) controlled experiments with ethnography-inspired observation, interviewing, and interpretation; and (3) forward exploration of intervention trajectories and downstream responses. CMASE reproduces reported statistical patterns and provides process-level records for mechanism-oriented interpretation. A controlled comparison shows that embedded social interventions leave observable, auditable processes that parametric manipulation does not. These findings highlight CMASE's methodological value for studying interventions in small- and medium-scale social settings and its interdisciplinary potential. The official code is available at: https://github.com/armihia/CMASE.

### Title: Towards Unified World Models for Visual Navigation via Memory-Augmented Planning and Foresight
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.08713

Abstract: Enabling embodied agents to imagine future states is essential for robust and generalizable visual navigation. Yet, state-of-the-art systems typically rely on modular designs that decouple navigation planning from visual world modeling, which often induces state-action misalignment and weak adaptability in novel or dynamic scenarios. We propose UniWM, a unified, memory-augmented world model that integrates egocentric visual foresight and planning within a single multimodal autoregressive backbone. UniWM explicitly grounds action selection in visually imagined outcomes, tightly aligning prediction with control. ### Title: How RLHF Amplifies Sycophancy
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01002
Summary: arXiv:2602.01002v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Large language models often exhibit increased sycophantic behavior after preference-based post-training, showing a stronger tendency to affirm a user's stated or implied belief even when this conflicts with factual accuracy or sound judgment. We present a formal analysis of how alignment from human feedback can increase this failure mode by identifying an explicit amplification mechanism that causally links optimization against a learned reward to bias in the human preference data used for alignment. We show that the direction of behavioral drift is determined by a covariance under the base policy between endorsing the belief signal in the prompt and the learned reward, and that the first-order effect reduces to a simple mean-gap condition. We then analyze reward learning from pairwise comparisons under random utility models like Bradley-Terry and characterize when bias in human annotators' preferences induces this reward gap. Next, we propose a training-time intervention designed to neutralize the amplification mechanism itself. Among all post-trained policies that prevent sycophantic behavior from increasing, we characterize the unique policy closest in KL divergence to the unconstrained post-trained policy, and derive the corresponding minimal reward correction as a closed-form agreement penalty. Computational experiments find that reward gaps are common and cause behavioral drift in all the configurations considered.

### Title: Moral Sycophancy in Vision Language Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.08311
Summary: arXiv:2602.08311v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Sycophancy in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) refers to their tendency to align with user opinions, often at the expense of moral or factual accuracy. While prior studies have explored sycophantic behavior in general contexts, its impact on morally grounded visual decision-making remains insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we present the first systematic study of moral sycophancy in VLMs, analyzing ten widely-used models on the Moralise and M^3oralBench datasets under explicit user disagreement. Our results reveal that VLMs frequently produce morally incorrect follow-up responses even when their initial judgments are correct, and exhibit a consistent asymmetry: models are more likely to shift from morally right to morally wrong judgments than the reverse when exposed to user-induced bias. Follow-up prompts generally degrade performance on Moralise, while yielding mixed or even improved accuracy on M^3oralBench, highlighting dataset-dependent differences in moral robustness. Evaluation using Error Introduction Rate (EIR) and Error Correction Rate (ECR) reveals a clear trade-off: models with stronger error-correction capabilities tend to introduce more reasoning errors, whereas more conservative models minimize errors but exhibit limited ability to self-correct. Finally, initial contexts with a morally right stance elicit stronger sycophantic behavior, emphasizing the vulnerability of VLMs to moral influence and the need for principled strategies to improve ethical consistency and robustness in multimodal AI systems.

### Title: Accurate and Efficient Multi-Channel Time Series Forecasting via Sparse Attention Mechanism
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18712
Summary: arXiv:2603.18712v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: The task of multi-channel time series forecasting is ubiquitous in numerous fields such as finance, supply chain management, and energy planning. It is critical to effectively capture complex dynamic dependencies within and between channels for accurate predictions. However, traditional method paid few attentions on learning the interaction among channels. This paper proposes Linear-Network (Li-Net), a novel architecture designed for multi-channel time series forecasting that captures the linear and non-linear dependencies among channels. Li-Net dynamically compresses representations across sequence and channel dimensions, processes the information through a configurable non-linear module and subsequently reconstructs the forecasts. Moreover, Li-Net integrates a sparse Top-K Softmax attention mechanism within a multi-scale projection framework to address these challenges. A core innovation is its ability to seamlessly incorporate and fuse multi-modal embeddings, guiding the sparse attention process to focus on the most informative time steps and feature channels. Through the experiment results on multiple real-world benchmark datasets demonstrate that Li-Net achieves competitive performance compared to state-of-the-art baseline methods. Furthermore, Li-Net provides a superior balance between prediction accuracy and computational burden, exhibiting significantly lower memory usage and faster inference times. Detailed ablation studies and parameter sensitivity analyses validate the effectiveness of each key component in our proposed architecture.
