### Title: Frozen Models, Evolving Expertise: Model-Agnostic Learning from Deployment Experience for Multimodal Medical AI
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09146
Summary: arXiv:2610.09146v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs) are usually frozen after deployment, so they do not learn from the cases they solve. This is especially concerning in medicine, where new clinical evidence, updated guidelines, and new therapies can change established practice. Fine-tuning can update the model, but it requires access to model weights and additional training. Parameter-free methods avoid training, but they may overfit a fixed validation set, lack reliable domain knowledge, or lose visual details by saving experience only as text. To address these limitations, we present a model-agnostic framework that allows frozen LLMs and VLMs to learn from deployment experience through three forms of external expertise: a Skill that guides reasoning and tool use, a Knowledge Memory that stores reliable facts supported by earlier cases or trusted external evidence, and a Multimodal Knowledge Base that keeps visual examples and guides the model to relate each retrieved case to the current image. Instead of relying on a fixed validation set, a validation strategy keeps an update only if it helps on new cases without degrading performance on earlier ones. Across six benchmarks covering clinical diagnosis, clinical workflows, medical reasoning, and medical and non-medical visual reasoning, and with four open-weight and closed-source base models, our framework improves performance during online deployment by up to 34.2% over the base model on medical tasks, generalizes to unseen cases, transfers to other models without further optimization, and works in non-medical domains.

### Title: RIT-RAG: Navigating Document Corpora with Retrieval-Induced Trees
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11370
Summary: arXiv:2610.11370v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) grounds language models in external corpora. Agentic RAG enables iterative search, yet exposes the model to isolated chunks without document structure, making it difficult to distinguish relevant evidence from chunks that merely resemble the query. Structure-aware methods such as PageIndex navigate using the structure but cannot scale to structures of large corpora that cannot fit in the LLM context. Hence, they first commit to a single document using a document retriever and cannot recover from a wrong choice. We propose RIT-RAG (Retrieval-Induced Tree RAG), which combines content retrieval with structural navigation. Offline, RIT-RAG builds a tree for each document from its table of contents or sitemap. At query time, it retrieves a broad set of chunks and uses their positions to induce manageable sub-trees, potentially across multiple documents. An LLM agent navigate these sub-trees, selectively reads promising nodes, and reformulates queries when needed. Thus, retrieval proposes where to look, while the agent decides what to read. Across financial, scientific, and customer-support benchmarks, RIT-RAG achieves the highest answer accuracy among vanilla, graph-based, and agentic baselines. On EntQABench, our new benchmark of 2.84 million technical-documentation webpages, it improves accuracy by 6.6--11.4 points over the strongest baseline across three LLMs.

### Title: Scaling to Tens of Thousands of Test-Time Iterations with Loop-Native Attention Residuals
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11570
Summary: arXiv:2610.11570v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: In this paper, we argue that Looped Transformers need their own residual connections to prevent performance degradation as the number of iterations grows. We first introduce an interesting phenomenon we observed in Looped Transformers, called latent overthinking: noisy state updates overwrite correct intermediate deductions and even undo completed solutions. This leaves subsequent iterations to recover lost information from an already degraded representation: once an error arises in an earlier loop, often as a result of long-range propagation through the recurrence, later loops find it difficult to correct. In this paper, we introduce InfiLoop, a loop-native residual connection that learns which past computations to retain and how much to accept from each new update. InfiLoop combines content-based weighting with learned temporal decay to maintain a running summary of recurrent states. An exact streaming recurrence keeps its persistent aggregation memory constant as the loop count grows. The resulting adaptive update suppresses unreliable proposals and preserves useful intermediate states. Across extensive reasoning tasks, a 7M-parameter InfiLoop model outperforms existing recursive architectures, reaching 97.9% exact accuracy on Sudoku-Extreme, and 13.6% pass@2 on ARC-AGI-2. Notably, on Sudoku-Extreme, InfiLoop continues to improve with test-time looping beyond 20,000 effective steps, showing that added depth translates directly into stronger reasoning. Our code is available at https://github.com/pixeli99/InfiLoop.

### Title: MultiWorldBench: Do Independently Controlled Views Describe One Shared World?
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11723
Summary: arXiv:2610.11723v3 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Multiplayer world models must ensure that independently controlled views remain consistent with a single shared and persistent world. We introduce MultiWorldBench, a diagnostic Minecraft benchmark comprising 495 case configurations across seven task suites and ten capabilities, including independent control, cross-view motion, shared-state synchronization, persistence, structural reasoning, concurrent interaction, and delayed revisit. Using Engine GT as a reference, we evaluate two multiplayer world models, Solaris (MP) and Gamma-World, together with a single-player generation variant, Solaris (SP). Among the generated systems, Gamma-World achieves the highest ten-capability average at 21.39, followed by Solaris (MP) at 20.88 and Solaris (SP) at 10.06, while Engine GT reaches 91.69. Gamma-World performs better on several control, shared-state, and revisit capabilities, whereas Solaris (MP) leads in cross-view motion and race-condition consistency. Nevertheless, all generated systems score at most 8.00 on state persistence and 1.33 on structural consistency, and none succeeds in spatial reasoning or building-identity preservation. Human pairwise evaluation yields the same overall system ranking and shows strong alignment with the automatic evaluation, with a mean dimension-level Spearman correlation of 0.95. These results show that current multiplayer world models still exhibit a substantial gap relative to Engine GT in maintaining a coherent shared world.

### Title: Safe Actions Alone Do Not Ensure Safe Agents: Identifying Unfulfilled Obligations with Guard Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.11773
Summary: arXiv:2610.11773v3 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Guard models are increasingly used to safeguard LLM-based agents, primarily by identifying actions that agents are forbidden to perform. However, identifying forbidden actions alone is insufficient to ensure agent safety. In this paper, we argue that agent safety also depends on identifying required yet unperformed safety-critical actions, which we call obligations. Our preliminary study on a popular benchmark for evaluating safety shows that 56.92% of GLM-5.3 trajectories contain unfulfilled obligations, compared with only 30.00% containing forbidden actions. This finding reveals unfulfilled obligations as a major and previously overlooked source of safety risk. However, to our knowledge, no existing benchmark evaluates whether guard models can identify these obligations. To close this gap, we introduce ObligationBench, the first benchmark for evaluating the capability of obligation identification, comprising 240 expert-validated trajectories covering issue resolution, feature development, and terminal operations. Our evaluation of 14 representative models reveals substantial limitations: the highest recall and exact-match rate are only 48.97% and 10.00%, respectively. To address these limitations, we develop ObligationGuard using 40,000 synthetic training examples. ObligationGuard achieves 57.52% recall and an exact-match rate of 21.67%, surpassing all evaluated models on both metrics. We call on the community to incorporate obligation identification into the design and evaluation of future guard models to improve agent safety.

### Title: Are Multimodal Benchmarks Really Useful? Item-Level Multimodal Benchmark Diagnosis via Structure-Response Co-Calibration
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14542
Summary: arXiv:2610.14542v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Multimodal benchmarks are central to evaluating whether models can reason over information from multiple modalities. However, existing literature has revealed that some benchmarks may contain shortcut items that can be answered from a single modality or from answer-option regularities alone, thus failing to reflect models' true cross-modal inference ability. Existing methods for assessing benchmarks typically rely either on intrinsic item structure or extrinsic model response behavior, which are not adequate for a comprehensive diagnosis. To fill in this gap, we propose SRCoD, a structure-response co-calibrated diagnosis framework for item-level diagnosis of modality dependence in multimodal benchmarks. SRCoD estimates sample-wise multimodal information structure from benchmark content and target answers, and uses this signal as a soft structural anchor for modality-conditioned item response modeling. The learned model produces a calibrated item-level diagnostic profile that reflects how strongly an item depends on different forms of modality evidence, enabling fine-grained benchmark analysis beyond aggregate accuracy. SRCoD also provides interpretable attribution of the diagnosis results. Extensive experiments under two complementary protocols, i.e., benchmark-refinement oriented validation and human-labeled item validation, demonstrate that SRCoD outperforms state-of-the-art benchmark diagnosis methods.

### Title: MemeLogos: Knowledge-Driven Retrieval-Augmented Reasoning for Harmful Meme Detection
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15463
Summary: arXiv:2610.15463v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: With the rapid proliferation of social media, harmful memes have become a growing threat to online ecosystems. Their harmfulness is often implicitly conveyed through complex interactions among visual, textual, and contextual cues, making accurate detection challenging. Recent studies have introduced Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) and external knowledge into harmful meme detection. However, existing methods mainly retrieve similar instances or auxiliary information, limiting their ability to capture reusable harmfulness knowledge and underlying reasoning mechanisms, and thus constraining both detection performance and interpretability. To address these challenges, we propose MemeLogos, a knowledge-driven retrieval-augmented reasoning framework for harmful meme detection. MemeLogos abstracts recurring harmfulness patterns and reasoning structures from diverse harmful cases into reusable knowledge units and organizes them into a dedicated star-shaped knowledge graph. By combining structured knowledge retrieval with an explicit multi-stage reasoning process inspired by cognitive mechanisms, MemeLogos leverages retrieved knowledge to guide harmfulness reasoning, improving both detection reliability and interpretability. Extensive experiments show that MemeLogos consistently outperforms existing harmful meme detection methods while generating more coherent and interpretable reasoning, demonstrating the effectiveness of structured harmfulness knowledge for multimodal content understanding.

### Title: Engram Injection: A Study of Temporal Validity in Parametric Language-Model Memory
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15882
Summary: arXiv:2610.15882v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: A stored fact may stop being valid when a user changes a preference or an event replaces an earlier one. We study whether a language model can learn these changes in its parameters and answer correctly from only a question and the current time. Engram Injection stores facts in LoRA adapters and supplies the clock through FiLM conditioning on a frozen Qwen2.5-3B-Instruct model. We compare it with additive clock conditioning, prompting with the full dated history, and retrieving four dated records. We train six matched adapters across three seeds after fixing a sampler that could admit test-age examples into training. ### Title: RoTA-Bench: Evaluating 3D Spatial Reasoning through Interactive Projection Alignment
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17665
Summary: arXiv:2610.17665v3 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Can vision-language models turn 2D observations into 3D understanding that supports precise action? This requires inferring object geometry, anticipating the effects of rotation, and refining actions through visual feedback. We introduce RoTA-Bench to evaluate these abilities through interactive 3D-to-2D projection alignment. Given a target silhouette, a model observes a rotated object and chooses successive rotations to recover the target projection within a limited action budget. Our automated pipeline constructs silhouette-preserving 3D objects from 2D icons and uses reversible initialization to guarantee solvability, yielding 674 tasks and a stratified 100-task test set. Empirically, we find that (i) a substantial human-model gap persists: GPT-5.6-Sol succeeds on only 17% of tasks, whereas 20 undergraduate participants collectively complete the test set four times with a 100% success rate; (ii) image-space and pose-space progress can diverge: at matched states, the model more often improves silhouette overlap while worsening pose alignment than humans (19.4% vs. 10.9%); and (iii) fine-grained control is sensitive to interaction history: removing it leaves 96.8% of the model's actions at a coarse 30-degree step size and sharply reduces observed task success. These findings highlight the challenge of converting visual evidence into precise 3D control, beyond recognizing or matching individual images.

### Title: Rethinking Quality in Large Language Model Routing: Security-Conditioned Model-and-Budget Selection
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18566
Summary: arXiv:2610.18566v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Large language model (LLM) routers reduce serving cost by choosing which model to call and how many output tokens to grant it for each query. Existing routers rank model-budget actions using a single quality metric. This does not work for mixed workloads. For ordinary queries, a good answer is correct and useful (utility); for security-sensitive queries, a good answer refuses harmful requests or resists malicious instructions (security). These two objectives prefer different models and budgets.
  We propose SCRouter, a router that learns when to prioritize security and how to allocate the token budget. For each model-budget pair, it estimates the probability that the response finishes within budget. It uses this probability to predict utility and security separately. A small probe determines how to weight these two predictions for each query. SCRouter then selects the model and budget by balancing the weighted utility and security against cost. We also build SCRouterBench, a benchmark of 38,869 utility and security queries spanning nine security dimensions, each answered under all 144 combinations of 12 models and 12 budgets, plus 2,753 out-of-domain queries. SCRouter improves the baselines by 13-29 points in-domain and 19-23 points out-of-domain.

Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20191
Summary: arXiv:2610.20191v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Medication recommendation (MR) is critical for clinical decision support. In multi center settings, abundant data from large medical centers can improve recommendation performance for low-resource centers. Despite a few preliminary attempts on this task, two critical gaps remain. Gap 1: Existing multi-center methods fail to disentangle transferable cross-hospital clinical experience from hospital-specific prescribing patterns, limiting clinical feasibility and physician acceptance. Gap 2: They commonly overlook multi label safety constraints of MR, including drug-drug interaction risks and local formulary feasibility, which are essential for safe real-world deployment. To address these gaps, we propose HEM-Med, a novel hierarchical agentic memory framework for multi-center MR. To address Gap 1, we design a disentangled hierarchical memory module that builds patient-level medication context attribution records and disentangles them into cross hospital experience memory and hospital-specific residual memory. To address Gap 2, we introduce a safety-aware medication set generation module, which integrates dual-level experience retrieval with safety-aware set decoding to produce clinically relevant, safer, and locally adaptive medication recommendations. Experiments on a real-world multi-center eICU dataset show that HEM-Med outperforms representative transfer learning, LLM-based, and MR baselines in terms of both recommendation accuracy and safety.

### Title: SafeAdapt-Poker: Counterfactual Risk Calibration for Budgeted Adaptation under Opponent Shift
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20473
Summary: arXiv:2610.20473v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Opponent histories can mislead after behavioral shifts. SafeAdapt-Poker formulates budgeted adaptation under opponent shift as a dual-counterfactual sequential-control problem using only information available before each decision. At each decision the controller asks two distinct questions: what local downside a deviation from the frozen anchor would induce under matched legal continuations, and what future adaptation value would be lost by consuming part of the remaining deviation budget now. The first question is answered by a monotone strategic-risk model over deviation intensity; the second by a paired budget-state continuation model that prices current budget consumption. The controller selects among support-preserving candidate deviations by trading immediate adaptation value against these two quantities, a pathwise cumulative-KL ledger bounds total anchor-relative deviation, and a finite family of frozen controllers is screened on complete paired calibration sessions before held-out interaction. A finite-candidate perturbation bound shows that the allocation objective degrades continuously with continuation-value estimation error. Evaluation covers exact-game exploitation and safety, four opponent shifts, local-risk calibration, delayed-opportunity allocation, direct opportunity-value estimation, value-error robustness, factorial mechanism attribution, budget sensitivity, abstract-HUNL cross-play, and player/time-held-out public-history behavior. On exact Leduc the full controller attains a matched weak-pool gain of +0.0718 with a worst-opponent delta of -0.0126, raises delayed-opportunity capture from 0.6318 under myopic allocation to 0.7841 under the same session budget, and reaches +42.7 mbb/hand in abstract-HUNL cross-play; the retained public-history screen reports 0.5820 action accuracy and 0.9277 log loss versus 0.5039 and 0.9778 for a public prior.

### Title: ActiveEvidence: Decision-Focused Evidence Graphs with Verifiable Acquisition Gaps
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20532
Summary: arXiv:2610.20532v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: A multimodal agent must decide whether visual evidence is worth acquiring, which candidate or resolution to query, and when further interaction can no longer change its decision. We study this problem under a strict free-metadata/paid-observation boundary, stochastic vector costs, and a frozen execution trace, and treat it as decision-certifiable active evidence acquisition. A Counterfactual Evidence Interaction Graph (CEIG) represents unary evidence value, signed second-order interaction, and directed conditional harm while maintaining explicit score enclosures for unresolved interactions; a decision-weighted enclosure objective tightens the bounds that can change action identity. Gap-directed refinement evaluates only interactions that can displace the incumbent acquisition and stops once an $\epsilon$-decision certificate shows that unresolved terms cannot change the selected action beyond a declared tolerance. The certificate is model-relative: it verifies policy-consistent optimality under the pinned learned score, and semantic quality is measured separately through post-freeze labels. Paired continuation rollouts learn the downstream value of paid previews, an online operational cost filter is separated from independent finite-policy-family trajectory certification, and an acquisition monitor with an independently implemented verifier binds every certificate to the frozen trace. On 640 cases split into 384/128/128 training, validation, and test cases, the controller reaches task score 0.842, grounding F1 0.816, evidence recall 0.873, 1.74 accepted calls, 286.5K visual tokens, and 742ms p95 latency. Dense replay of issued certificates shows 0.992 action agreement and a 0.988 certificate rate while evaluating 19.6 instead of 191.4 expensive interaction terms per decision.

### Title: When Reasoning Traces Conflict with Evidence: How Answer Configuration Shapes Evidence Following
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20805
Summary: arXiv:2610.20805v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: A reasoning trace retained at answer time plays two roles: it can raise accuracy, and it can compete with the current evidence when the two disagree. We disentangle these roles by crossing verified evidence versions with the traces generated from each and answering from the same traces and evidence under two configurations: continuation of the model's own thinking and a separate answer call. Across three model families (gpt-oss, Gemma4, Qwen3) on HotpotQA and MuSiQue, the separate call raises evidence-following over continuation by 29.9 to 60.6 points. On the same pairs, removing the trace lowers accuracy by similar amounts under both configurations. Yet continuation repeats the stale conclusion in most conflicts, and the separate call follows the current evidence far more often. Benefiting from a trace and deferring to it are therefore distinct properties. Further analyses show that the final line of a trace carries most of its value, gold evidence helps most before the trace is written, distractors weaken correction under a separate call, and renewed reasoning, unlike a verification instruction, follows the current evidence in most conflicts. Because trace deletion alone cannot reveal which source an answer follows, evaluations should report both properties together with the answer configuration.

### Title: All-in-One Memory: Process-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Memory Agents
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21147
Summary: arXiv:2610.21147v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Large language models increasingly rely on external memory to answer queries grounded in long-horizon histories such as multi-session dialogues and lengthy documents. However, existing memory methods often optimize retrieval and memory construction separately, without a shared reward space for evaluating search, writing, and stopping decisions. To address this, we propose All-in-One Memory (AiO-Mem), which organizes these operations as transitions over a shared evidence state and directly evaluates the semantic quality of each action in a unified reward space. AiO-Mem compiles heterogeneous histories into source-traceable multi-view events, providing a shared substrate for retrieval and memory writing. A unified memory policy actor then rolls out complete interaction trajectories over this shared evidence state. A separate trajectory-conditioned process reward model is trained through evidence-grounded supervised fine-tuning and trajectory-level DPO to assign process scores to the collected trajectories. These scores are aggregated into an action-balanced trajectory reward and used by Process-Aligned DAPO (PA-DAPO) to optimize the memory policy. Across long-context memory benchmarks, AiO-Mem consistently improves answer accuracy over both static memory systems and RL-trained memory agents.

### Title: Jev-LiteMAS: Separating Decisions from Generation in Multi-Agent Workflows
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21411
Summary: arXiv:2610.21411v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Multi-agent LLM systems rely on generative models for expert execution, workflow planning, and artifact review. Yet many coordination operations require only bounded decisions, such as selecting the next expert or judging artifact quality. Profiling three representative MAS frameworks, we find that routing and review account for 31.7-43.5% of generative-LLM tokens. However, replacing these operations with low-cost decision models introduces two interface gaps: generative planners produce execution plans and handoff instructions (planning gap), while reviewers provide actionable feedback for repairing flawed artifacts (review gap), which bounded decisions alone cannot provide. We introduce Jev-LiteMAS, a multi-agent framework that bridges both gaps by combining a lightweight decision model (Jev) with generative execution and feedback. To address the planning gap, Jev selects experts dynamically from the task and prior expert outputs, using accumulated context to ground subsequent execution without a pre-generated trajectory. To address the review gap, Jev screens intermediate artifacts against task criteria: passing artifacts proceed directly, whereas flagged ones undergo LLM re-review for acceptance or corrective feedback. Across four benchmarks and two backbones, Jev-LiteMAS reduces generative-LLM tokens by 12.7-54.6% over LAS, maintaining accuracy within 1.34 percentage points and lowering latency in seven of eight settings. Grounding routing in substantive expert outputs prevents up to 18.0 points in accuracy loss.

### Title: Prism-1: Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22066
Summary: arXiv:2610.22066v2 Announce Type: replace 
Abstract: Given a state and a set of options, such as intents, tools or interface elements, System One decision models return a distribution over the options in one forward pass, without generated reasoning. Web systems increasingly build them by reading decisions from language models. Two axioms of rational choice are natural requirements for such decisions, because in web systems the listing and the option set are determined by other components and can change or be manipulated: the order in which the options are listed should not matter, and adding an option should not change the relative odds of the others (Luce's choice axiom). We test the standard reads, namely single-choice question (SCQ) prompting, with shuffled-order training, order averaging or sorted options, and invariant set architectures, and find that each violates at least one axiom: one shuffle changes 12-20% of SCQ answers, sorting only freezes an arbitrary convention, and a well-chosen added option steers 4-34% of decisions. By Luce's theorem, a read satisfies both axioms only if it is a softmax over scores that each depend on one option alone, so in a transformer they hold by construction only if no token that scores an option sees another. We implement this as Prism-1 (Position- and Rival-Independent Scoring for System One Models): every option is read in its own block at shared positions, with the model's usual left-to-right attention, in one forward pass. On five benchmark sets with Qwen3-8B and Gemma 4 12B, SCQ prompting trained on the same data still changes 7% of its answers under one shuffle, and an added irrelevant option steers 12% of its decisions. Prism-1 stays within one point of that baseline's accuracy and is 2-4 points more accurate once only answers that survive a reordering are counted, while no shuffle changes any of its answers and no added irrelevant option moves its choice among the others.

### Title: Harnessing Trust in Directed Graphs: Redefining Robustness of Graph Learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2306.02002
Summary: arXiv:2306.02002v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Existing research on robust Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) focuses predominantly on undirected graphs, overlooking the trustworthiness inherent in the link directions of directed graphs. In this work, we analyze the limitations of current approaches from both the attack and defense perspectives and redefine the robustness of GNNs in directed graphs. From the attack side, we introduce a more realistic directed graph attack setting that addresses the shortcomings of existing attacks. From the defense side, we propose a simple yet effective message-passing framework that serves as a plug-in layer to strengthen GNN robustness while avoiding a false sense of robustness. Our findings show that the trust signals encoded in directed graphs can be harnessed to substantially improve the robustness of GNNs. When combined with existing defense strategies, the framework achieves state-of-the-art robust performance alongside strong clean accuracy against both transfer and adaptive attacks. These results point to a novel and promising direction for strengthening the robustness of graph deep learning.

### Title: Beyond Boundaries: A Comprehensive Survey of Transferable Attacks on AI Systems
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2311.11796
Summary: arXiv:2311.11796v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Transferable attacks allow adversarial manipulations or learned attack mechanisms constructed in one setting to compromise other AI systems, potentially reducing attack costs and amplifying security and privacy risks. Yet fragmented terminology and evaluation practices hinder understanding of what transfers, why it transfers, and where transfer fails. This survey synthesizes representative research and develops a unified taxonomy spanning six complementary axes: cross-instance, cross-domain, cross-modality, cross-model, cross-task, and cross-deployment. By separating source-target configuration changes from attacker access and adaptation budgets, the taxonomy provides a common framework for comparing transfer across evasion, poisoning, backdoors, privacy inference, model stealing, and prompt-based attacks. We connect transfer mechanisms, including gradient alignment, shared representations, training persistence, and deployment invariances, to enhancement methods based on augmentation, disentanglement, and optimization. Our synthesis identifies inconsistent transfer, incompatible metrics, and gaps between simulated and physical evaluations, and establishes a research agenda for compositional transfer, configuration differences, temporal persistence, and reproducible benchmarking. Together, this work supports systematic investigation of shared vulnerabilities and guides transfer-aware security assessment and defense.

### Title: Paint Outside the Box: Synthesizing and Selecting Training Data for Visual Grounding
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.00684
Summary: arXiv:2412.00684v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Visual grounding aims to localize the image regions based on a textual query. To address the data scarcity, we propose a novel framework, POBF (Paint Outside the Box and Filter). POBF synthesizes images by inpainting outside the box, tackling a label misalignment issue encountered in previous works. ### Title: Rethinking Negative Prompting for Concept Suppression: Unconditional Anchoring and Localized Guidance
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2412.07658
Summary: arXiv:2412.07658v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Text-to-image diffusion models have rapidly transitioned from research prototypes to widely deployed tools. Along with their impressive capabilities, these models can also generate harmful or undesirable content (e.g., NSFW imagery), motivating practical mechanisms for removing such concepts. Negative prompting, which uses the negative-prompt prediction as the guidance anchor, is among the most widely used mechanisms for steering sampling trajectories away from unwanted concepts. However, for jailbreak prompts, it can fail in a regime we refer to as \emph{guidance collapse}, where the model's denoising predictions for the prompt and negative prompt become similar, so that the guidance difference driving negative prompting vanishes and the guided prediction degenerates to the negative-prompt prediction, undermining suppression. To fix this issue, we propose TraSCE (Trajectory Steering for ConcEpt Suppression), a training-free inference-time method that makes negative prompting reliable under guidance collapse. TraSCE combines two complementary components: (i) unconditional anchoring, which makes the fallback under guidance collapse a neutral unconditional prediction rather than the negative-prompt prediction, and (ii) an alignment-localized steering step, which deliberately induces this collapse for adversarial prompts and steers the sample to the unconditional fallback, while remaining minimally intrusive on benign prompts. We evaluate TraSCE on adversarial prompt benchmarks targeting nudity and violence, and further demonstrate its applicability to suppressing artistic styles and objects. Across these settings, TraSCE substantially improves the robustness of negative prompting to jailbreak prompts without any training, weight updates, or pre-collected concept-specific data (prompts or images), while keeping the impact on general image quality small.

### Title: What is causal about causal models and representations?
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2501.19335
Summary: arXiv:2501.19335v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Causal Bayesian networks are often described as causal because they support reasoning about interventions. Yet interventions are defined in the mathematical model, whereas the resulting model predictions concern the effects of actions in the real world. Consequently, whether and how a causal model makes falsifiable predictions about the effects of actions depends on how actions are interpreted as interventions. This requires specifying which actions correspond to which interventions, a step that is frequently left implicit. We develop a formal framework that separates a causal model from the data-generating process it aims to describe and formalizes interpretations connecting actions to interventions. Within this framework, we prove an impossibility result: no interpretation can simultaneously satisfy a collection of desirable properties while remaining non-circular. Under a circular interpretation, every model that fits the observational distribution is interventionally valid, so no experiment can falsify it. We analyze several non-circular alternatives and discuss implications for falsification, causal discovery, causal representation learning, and causal abstraction. More broadly, our framework provides a way to make assumptions about the connections between actions and interventions explicit and to reason formally about the empirical content of causal models.

### Title: LeanTTA: A Backpropagation-Free and Stateless Approach to Quantized Test-Time Adaptation on Edge Devices
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.15889
Summary: arXiv:2503.15889v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: While there are many advantages to deploying machine learning models on edge devices, the resource constraints of mobile platforms, the dynamic nature of the environment, and differences between the distribution of training versus in-the-wild data make such deployments challenging. Current test-time adaptation methods are often memory-intensive and not designed to be quantization-compatible or deployed on low-resource devices. To address these challenges, we present LeanTTA, a novel backpropagation-free and stateless framework for quantized test-time adaptation tailored to edge devices. Our approach minimizes computational costs by dynamically updating normalization statistics without backpropagation, which frees LeanTTA from the common pitfall of relying on large batches and historical data, making our method robust to realistic deployment scenarios. Our approach is the first to enable further computational gains by combining partial adaptation with quantized module fusion. We validate our framework across sensor modalities, demonstrating significant improvements over state-of-the-art TTA methods, including a 15.7% error reduction, peak memory usage of only 11.2MB for ResNet18, and fast adaptation within an order-of-magnitude of normal inference speeds on-device. LeanTTA provides a robust solution for achieving the right trade offs between accuracy and system efficiency in edge deployments, addressing the unique challenges posed by limited data and varied operational conditions.

### Title: Collaborating with AI Agents: Field Experiments on Teamwork, Productivity, and Performance
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.18238
Summary: arXiv:2503.18238v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: We examined the mechanisms underlying productivity and performance gains from AI agents using a large-scale experiment on Pairit, a platform we developed to study human-AI collaboration. We randomly assigned 2,234 participants to human-human and human-AI teams that produced 11,024 ads. We evaluated the ads using independent human ratings and a field experiment on X which garnered ~5M impressions. We found human-AI teams produced 50% more ads per worker and higher text quality, while human-human teams produced higher image quality, suggesting a jagged frontier of AI agent capability. Human-AI teams also produced more homogeneous outputs (diversity collapse) as their ads were more self-similar. The field experiment revealed higher text quality (produced by human-AI teams) increased click-through rates and view time, while higher image quality (produced by human-human teams) improved cost-per-click rates. Two mechanisms explained these effects: Human-AI collaboration was significantly more task-oriented and displayed more delegation. AI-appropriate work processes, in turn, improved performance in human-AI teams and transcended the jagged frontier: Task-oriented communication produced higher productivity, higher quality ads across all dimensions, and displayed no diversity collapse in their outputs, while human-AI teams with more interpersonal communication were less productive and produced lower quality ads across all dimensions. In contrast, in human-human teams, interpersonal communication improved productivity. Delegation improved text quality in human-AI teams and was positively associated with diversity collapse, creating homogeneous outputs of higher average quality. The results suggest AI agents drive productivity, performance, and output diversity by reshaping teamwork along the jagged frontier of AI capabilities.

### Title: A Gradient-Optimized TSK Fuzzy Framework for Explainable Phishing Detection
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2504.18636
Summary: arXiv:2504.18636v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Phishing attacks represent an increasingly sophisticated and pervasive threat to individuals and organizations, causing significant financial losses, identity theft, and severe damage to institutional reputations. Existing phishing detection methods often struggle to simultaneously achieve high accuracy and explainability, either failing to detect novel attacks or operating as opaque black-box models. To address this critical gap, we propose a novel phishing URL detection system based on a first-order Takagi-Sugeno-Kang (TSK) fuzzy inference model optimized through gradient-based techniques. Our approach intelligently combines the interpretability and human-like reasoning capabilities of fuzzy logic with the precision and adaptability provided by gradient optimization methods, specifically leveraging the Adam optimizer for efficient parameter tuning. Experiments conducted using a comprehensive dataset of over 235,000 URLs demonstrate rapid convergence, exceptional predictive performance (accuracy averaging 99.95% across 5 cross-validation folds, with a perfect AUC i.e. 1.00). Furthermore, optimized fuzzy rules and membership functions improve interoperability, clearly indicating how the model makes decisions - an essential feature for cybersecurity applications. This high-performance, transparent, and interpretable phishing detection framework significantly advances current cybersecurity defenses, providing practitioners with accurate and explainable decision-making tools.

### Title: Two Minds Better Than One: Collaborative Reward Modeling for LLM Alignment
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.10597
Summary: arXiv:2505.10597v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Reward models (RMs) are central to aligning large language models (LLMs) with human values, yet noisy preference feedback can induce reward misgeneralization through spurious correlations and overfitting. We characterize the training dynamics of preference pairs and uncover a distinctive optimization signature of noise: persistently high losses, sharp temporal fluctuations, and irregular gradient updates. Our analyses further show that excluding such pairs improves downstream alignment. In practice, however, reliable preferences are not known a priori, and self-filtering based on a model's own training signals can amplify confirmation bias. To address this challenge, we introduce Collaborative Reward Modeling (CRM), an online framework for identifying reliable preferences. CRM couples cross-model peer review with curriculum learning: two RMs evaluate each other's sample selections, while curriculum keeps their learning progress synchronized. Across extensive experiments, CRM improves RM generalization by up to 9.94 points on RewardBench under 40% preference noise. CRM further generalizes to implicit-reward alignment, offering a robust and broadly applicable approach to learning from imperfect feedback.

### Title: Safety Alignment via Constrained Knowledge Unlearning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.18588
Summary: arXiv:2505.18588v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Despite significant progress in safety alignment, large language models (LLMs) remain susceptible to jailbreak attacks. Existing defense mechanisms have not fully deleted harmful knowledge in LLMs, which allows such attacks to bypass safeguards and produce harmful outputs. To address this challenge, we propose a novel safety alignment strategy, Constrained Knowledge Unlearning (CKU), which focuses on two primary objectives: knowledge localization and retention, and unlearning harmful knowledge. CKU works by scoring neurons in specific multilayer perceptron (MLP) layers to identify a subset U of neurons associated with useful knowledge. During the unlearning process, CKU prunes the gradients of neurons in U to preserve valuable knowledge while effectively mitigating harmful content. Experimental results demonstrate that CKU significantly enhances model safety without compromising overall performance, offering a superior balance between safety and utility compared to existing methods. Additionally, our analysis of neuron knowledge sensitivity across various MLP layers provides valuable insights into the mechanics of safety alignment and model knowledge editing.

### Title: GPT, But Backwards: Exactly Inverting Language Model Outputs
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.01693
Summary: arXiv:2507.01693v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Recent work shows that distinct inputs produce distinct hidden representations in LLMs, so the input can in principle be recovered from them. Yet, no existing method can reliably do so from the model's output text or logits alone. We introduce Sparse One-hot Discrete Adam (SODA), a white-box search-based algorithm that reconstructs textual inputs from partial knowledge of the output. Given output logits, SODA recovers natural sequences of up to 17 tokens with zero false positives and achieves a 4x improvement in exact-match reconstruction over the strongest prior method. We further show that input length and vocabulary size matter more for inversion difficulty than parameter count, with SODA inverting models from 33M to 3B parameters. Finally, we demonstrate proof-of-concept security applications: SODA recovers 6 backdoor triggers from a poisoned LLM in under 1000 iterations, and 139 tokens of personally identifiable information from synthetic user-chatbot interactions. Our code is available at https://github.com/AdrSkapars/inversion_optimisation.

### Title: Missing value imputation with adversarial random forests - MissARF
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15681
Summary: arXiv:2507.15681v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Handling missing values is a common challenge in biostatistical analyses, typically addressed by imputation methods. We propose a novel, fast, and easy-to-use imputation method called missing value imputation with adversarial random forests (MissARF), based on generative machine learning, that provides both single and multiple imputation. MissARF employs adversarial random forest (ARF) for density estimation and data synthesis. To impute a missing value of an observation, we condition on the non-missing values and sample from the estimated conditional distribution generated by ARF. Our experiments demonstrate that MissARF performs comparably to state-of-the-art single and multiple imputation methods in terms of imputation quality and fast runtime with no additional costs for multiple imputation.

### Title: Cross-Layer Attention Probing for Fine-Grained Hallucination Detection
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09700
Summary: arXiv:2509.09700v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: With the large-scale adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) in various applications, there is a growing reliability concern due to their tendency to generate inaccurate text, i.e. hallucinations. In this work, we propose Cross-Layer Attention Probing (CLAP), a novel activation probing technique for hallucination detection, which processes the LLM activations across the entire residual stream as a joint sequence. Our empirical evaluations using five LLMs and three tasks show that CLAP improves hallucination detection compared to baselines on both greedy decoded responses as well as responses sampled at higher temperatures, thus enabling fine-grained detection, i.e. the ability to disambiguate hallucinations and non-hallucinations among different sampled responses to a given prompt. ### Title: Rep2Text: Decoding Full Text from a Single LLM Token Representation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.06571
Summary: arXiv:2511.06571v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Large language models represent text as hidden numerical patterns, but what information these representations actually contain remains a mystery. Can we recover the original text from just one of them? In this paper, we introduce Rep2Text, a method designed to answer this question by reconstructing text from a single hidden representation. We find that these representations preserve much more information than we might expect. For short texts of about 16 words, Rep2Text can recover roughly half of the original words while preserving their overall meaning. As the text gets longer, recovering the exact wording becomes harder, but the main ideas can still be recovered. These findings offer a new way to understand what language models store internally and highlight potential privacy risks, as hidden representations may reveal parts of the original input.

### Title: Operon: Incremental Construction of Ragged Data via Named Dimensions
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.16080
Summary: arXiv:2511.16080v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Modern data processing workflows frequently encounter ragged data: collections with variable-length elements that arise naturally in domains like natural language processing, scientific measurements, and autonomous AI agents. Existing workflow engines lack native support for tracking the shapes and dependencies inherent to ragged data, forcing users to manage complex indexing and dependency bookkeeping manually. We present Operon, a Rust-based workflow engine that addresses these challenges through a novel formalism of named dimensions with explicit dependency relations. Operon provides a domain-specific language where users declare pipelines with dimension annotations that are statically verified for correctness, while the runtime system dynamically schedules tasks as data shapes are incrementally discovered during execution. We formalize the mathematical foundation for reasoning about partial shapes and prove that Operon's incremental construction algorithm guarantees deterministic and confluent execution in parallel settings. The system's explicit modeling of partially-known states enables robust persistence and recovery mechanisms, while its per-task multi-queue architecture achieves efficient parallelism across heterogeneous task types. Empirical evaluation demonstrates that Operon outperforms an existing workflow engine with 14.94x baseline overhead reduction while maintaining near-linear end-to-end output rates as workloads scale, making it particularly suitable for large-scale data generation pipelines in machine learning applications.

### Title: AQUA-Net: Adaptive Frequency Fusion and Illumination Aware Network for Underwater Image Enhancement
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05960
Summary: arXiv:2512.05960v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Underwater images often suffer from severe color distortion, low contrast, and a hazy appearance due to wavelength-dependent light absorption and scattering. To address these challenges, this paper presents a novel underwater image enhancement model, called Adaptive Frequency Fusion and Illumination Aware Network (AQUA-Net). It integrates a residual encoder-decoder with dual auxiliary branches, which operate in the frequency and illumination domains. The frequency fusion encoder enriches spatial representations with frequency cues from the Fourier domain and preserves fine textures and structural details. Inspired by Retinex, the illumination-aware decoder performs adaptive exposure correction through a learned illumination map that separates reflectance from lighting effects. This joint spatial, frequency, and illumination design enables the model to restore color balance, visual contrast, and perceptual realism under diverse underwater conditions. Additionally, we present a high-resolution, real-world underwater video-derived dataset from the Mediterranean Sea, which captures challenging deep-sea conditions with realistic visual degradations to enable robust evaluation and development of deep learning models. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmark datasets show that AQUA-Net performs on par with SOTA in both qualitative and quantitative evaluations while using less number of parameters. Ablation studies further confirm that the frequency and illumination branches provide complementary contributions that improve visibility and color representation. Overall, the proposed model shows strong generalization capability and robustness, and it provides an effective solution for real-world underwater imaging applications.

### Title: Schr\"odinger's Navigator: Imagining an Ensemble of Futures for Zero-Shot Object Navigation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.21201
Summary: arXiv:2512.21201v5 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Zero-shot object navigation (ZSON) requires robots to find target objects in unseen environments without task-specific fine-tuning or pre-built maps, a key capability for general-purpose service robots. Yet methods that perform well in simulation often degrade in cluttered real-world scenes with severe occlusion and latent hazards, where large unseen regions make single-scene inference brittle and unsafe. We propose Schr\"odinger's Navigator, a belief-aware framework that reasons at inference time over multiple trajectory-conditioned imagined 3D futures. Given candidate paths, a trajectory-conditioned 3D world model predicts hypothetical observations and maintains a superposition of plausible scene realizations rather than committing to one map. An adaptive occluder-aware sampler directs imagination to uncertainty-critical regions, while a Future-Aware Value Map (FAVM) aggregates imagined futures for robust, proactive action selection. Experiments in simulation and on a physical Go2 quadruped show that Schr\"odinger's Navigator outperforms strong ZSON baselines, improving hidden-target discovery and risk-aware waypoint selection in occlusion-heavy navigation scenarios. These results highlight imagined 3D futures as a scalable and generalizable strategy for zero-shot navigation in uncertain real-world environments.

### Title: AI-exposed jobs deteriorated before ChatGPT
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02554
Summary: arXiv:2601.02554v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Public debate links worsening job prospects for AI-exposed occupations to the release of ChatGPT in late 2022. Using monthly U.S. unemployment insurance records, we measure occupation- and location-specific unemployment risk and find that risk rose in AI-exposed occupations beginning in early 2022, months before ChatGPT was released. Analyzing millions of LinkedIn profiles, we show that graduate cohorts from 2021 onward entered AI-exposed jobs at lower rates than earlier cohorts, with gaps opening before late 2022. Finally, from millions of university syllabi, we find that graduates taking more LLM-exposed curricula had shorter job searches after ChatGPT and higher first-job pay when entering highly exposed occupations. ### Title: Pro-AI Bias in Large Language Models
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.13749
Summary: arXiv:2601.13749v2 Announce Type: replace-cross

### Title: Happy Young Women, Grumpy Old Men? Emotion-Driven Demographic Biases in Synthetic Face Generation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00032
Summary: arXiv:2602.00032v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Synthetic faces from text-to-image (T2I) models pervade digital media, yet their demographic biases under emotionally conditioned prompts remain poorly understood. We aim to systematically audit how emotionally conditioned prompts affect demographic and perceived-attractiveness biases in synthetic faces generated by T2I models, with particular attention to intersectional patterns and cross-ecosystem differences across model families. We audited eight (4 Western and 4 Chinese) T2I models and generated 56,000 faces under seven prompt conditions: a neutral baseline and six emotion conditions. This produces a valence-driven mapping that is also associated with lower perceived attractiveness in generated faces. These findings indicate that demographic bias in T2I face generation is both pervasive and shaped by emotional conditioning. They underscore the need for intersectional, emotion-conditioned, and multilingual demographic audits as part of standard pre-deployment evaluation practices.

### Title: Inter- and Intra-Subject Variability in EEG: A Systematic Survey
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01019
Summary: arXiv:2602.01019v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) underpins neuroscience, clinical neurophysiology, and brain-computer interfaces (BCIs), yet pronounced inter- and intra-subject variability limits reliability, reproducibility, and translation. This systematic review studies that quantified or modeled EEG variability across resting-state, event-related potentials (ERPs), and task-related/BCI paradigms (including motor imagery and SSVEP) in healthy and clinical cohorts. Across paradigms, inter-subject differences are typically larger than within-subject fluctuations, but both affect inference and model generalization. Stability is feature-dependent: alpha-band measures and individual alpha peak frequency are often relatively reliable, whereas higher-frequency and many connectivity-derived metrics show more heterogeneous reliability; ERP reliability varies by component, with P300 measures frequently showing moderate-to-good stability. We summarize major sources of variability (biological, state-related, technical, and analytical), review common quantification and modeling approaches (e.g., ICC, CV, SNR, generalizability theory, and multivariate/learning-based methods), and provide recommendations for study design, reporting, and harmonization. Overall, EEG variability should be treated as both a practical constraint to manage and a meaningful signal to leverage for precision neuroscience and robust neurotechnology.

### Title: A Theoretical Analysis of Test-Driven Code Generation
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06098
Summary: arXiv:2602.06098v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Code assistants are increasingly utilized in test-driven software development, yet the theoretical mechanisms behind their environment-interaction strategies remain underexplored. First, we formalize several well-established selection heuristics as environment-aware estimators of code correctness. ### Title: Test-Time Scaling with Diffusion Language Models via Reward-Guided Stitching
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22871
Summary: arXiv:2602.22871v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Reasoning with large language models often benefits from generating multiple chains-of-thought, but existing aggregation strategies are typically trajectory-level (e.g., selecting the best trace or voting on the final answer), discarding useful intermediate work from partial or "nearly correct" attempts. We propose Stitching Noisy Diffusion Thoughts, a self-consistency framework that turns cheap diffusion-sampled reasoning into a reusable pool of step-level candidates. Given a problem, we (i) sample many diverse, low-cost reasoning trajectories using a masked diffusion language model, (ii) score every intermediate step with an off-the-shelf process reward model (PRM), and (iii) stitch these highest-quality steps across trajectories into a composite rationale. This rationale is then used to recompute only the final answer. This modular pipeline separates exploration (diffusion) from evaluation and solution synthesis, avoiding monolithic unified hybrids while preserving broad search. Across math reasoning benchmarks, we find that step-level recombination is most beneficial on harder problems, and ablations highlight the importance of the final solver in converting stitched but imperfect rationales into accurate answers. Using low-confidence diffusion sampling with parallel, independent rollouts, our training-free framework improves average accuracy by up to 23.8% across six math and coding tasks. At the same time, it achieves up to a 1.8x latency reduction relative to both traditional diffusion models (e.g., Dream, LLaDA) and unified architectures (e.g., TiDAR). The code is available https://github.com/roymiles/diffusion-stitching

### Title: HydroShear: Hydroelastic Shear Simulation for Tactile Sim-to-Real Reinforcement Learning

Summary: arXiv:2603.00446v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: In this paper, we address the problem of tactile sim-to-real policy transfer for contact-rich tasks. Consequently, these models exhibit a large sim-to-real gap for many dexterous tasks. Here, we present HydroShear, a non-holonomic hydroelastic tactile simulator that advances the state-of-the-art by modeling: a) stick-slip transitions, b) path-dependent force and shear build up, and c) full SE(3) object-sensor interactions. Our method achieves a 93% average success rate, outperforming policies trained on tactile images (34%) and alternative shear simulation methods (58%-61%).

### Title: Language Model Goal Selection Differs from Humans' in a Self-Directed Learning Task
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03295
Summary: arXiv:2603.03295v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Whether in agentic workflows, social studies, or chat settings, large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being asked to replace humans in choosing which goals to pursue, rather than completing predefined tasks. However, the assumption that LLMs accurately reflect human preferences for goal setting remains largely untested. We assess the validity of LLMs as proxies for human goal selection in a controlled, self-directed learning task borrowed from cognitive science. Across five models (GPT-5, Gemini 2.5 Pro, Claude Sonnet 4.5, Qwen3 32B, and Centaur), we find substantial divergence from human behavior. ### Title: AOI: Turning Failed Trajectories into Training Signals for Autonomous Cloud Diagnosis
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03378
Summary: arXiv:2603.03378v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents offer a promising data-driven approach to automating Site Reliability Engineering (SRE), yet their enterprise deployment is constrained by three challenges: restricted access to proprietary data, unsafe action execution under permission-governed environments, and the inability of closed systems to improve from failures. We present AOI (Autonomous Operations Intelligence), a trainable multi-agent framework formulating automated operations as a structured trajectory learning problem under security constraints. Our approach integrates three key components. First, a trainable diagnostic system applies Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) to distill expert-level knowledge into locally deployed open-source models, enabling preference-based learning without exposing sensitive data. Second, a read-write separated execution architecture decomposes operational trajectories into observation, reasoning, and action phases, allowing safe learning while preventing unauthorized state mutation. Third, a Failure Trajectory Closed-Loop Evolver mines unsuccessful trajectories and converts them into corrective supervision signals, enabling continual data augmentation. Evaluated on the AIOpsLab benchmark, our contributions yield cumulative gains. (1) The AOI runtime alone achieves 66.3% best@5 success on all 86 tasks, outperforming the prior state-of-the-art (41.9%) by 24.4 points. (2) Adding Observer GRPO training, a locally deployed 14B model reaches 42.9% avg@1 on 63 held-out tasks with unseen fault types, surpassing Claude Sonnet 4.5. (3) The Evolver converts 37 failed trajectories into diagnostic guidance, improving end-to-end avg@5 by 4.8 points while reducing variance by 35%.

### Title: M$^2$RNN: Non-Linear RNNs with Matrix-Valued States for Scalable Language Modeling
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14360
Summary: arXiv:2603.14360v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Transformers are highly parallel but are limited to computations in the TC$^0$ complexity class, excluding tasks such as entity tracking and code execution that provably require greater expressive power. Motivated by this limitation, we revisit non-linear Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) for language modeling and introduce Matrix-to-Matrix RNN (M$^2$RNN): an architecture with matrix-valued hidden states and expressive non-linear state transitions. We demonstrate that the language modeling performance of non-linear RNNs is limited by their state size, and show how the state size expansion mechanism enables efficient use of tensor cores. Empirically, M$^2$RNN achieves perfect state tracking generalization at sequence lengths not seen during training. These benefits also translate to large-scale language modeling. In hybrid settings that interleave recurrent layers with attention, Hybrid M$^2$RNN outperforms equivalent Gated DeltaNet hybrids by $0.4$-$0.5$ perplexity points on a 7B MoE model, while using $3\times$ smaller state sizes for the recurrent layers. Notably, replacing even a single recurrent layer with M$^2$RNN in an existing hybrid architecture yields accuracy gains comparable to Hybrid M$^2$RNN with minimal impact on training throughput. Further, the Hybrid Gated DeltaNet models with a single M$^2$RNN layer also achieve superior long-context generalization, outperforming state-of-the-art hybrid linear attention architectures by up to $8$ points on LongBench. Together, these results establish non-linear RNN layers as a compelling building block for efficient and scalable language models.

### Title: OxyGen: Unified KV Cache Management for VLA Inference under Multi-Task Parallelism
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.14371
Summary: arXiv:2603.14371v5 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Embodied AI agents increasingly require parallel execution of multiple tasks, such as manipulation, conversation, and memory construction, from shared observations under distinct time constraints. Recent Mixture-of-Transformers (MoT) Vision-Language-Action Models (VLAs) support such heterogeneous outputs, yet existing inference systems incur redundant computation and resource contention when serving concurrent tasks on-device. We identify isolated KV cache management as the root cause. To address this, we propose unified KV cache management, an inference design that treats the KV cache as a first-class shared resource across tasks and control cycles. This abstraction enables two key optimizations: cross-task KV sharing eliminates redundant prefill of shared observations, while cross-frame continuous batching batches unfinished language requests from different frames and advances them within a per-frame decoding budget. We implement this design in OxyGen and evaluate $\pi_{0.5}$ on NVIDIA GeForce RTX 4090 and Jetson AGX Thor. OxyGen achieves up to 3.7$\times$ speedup over isolated execution, delivering over 200 tokens/s language throughput and 70 Hz action frequency simultaneously on RTX 4090. We also validate the design on Xiaomi-Robotics-0 and StarVLA across different backbones and expert configurations. We further demonstrate the inference gains on a humanoid robot with on-board Jetson AGX Thor.

### Title: Understanding and Defending VLM Jailbreaks via Jailbreak-Related Representation Shift
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.17372
Summary: arXiv:2603.17372v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Large vision-language models (VLMs) often exhibit weakened safety alignment with the integration of the visual modality. Even when text prompts contain explicit harmful intent, adding an image can substantially increase jailbreak success rates. In this paper, we observe that VLMs can clearly distinguish benign inputs from harmful ones in their representation space. Moreover, even among harmful inputs, jailbreak samples form a distinct internal state that is separable from refusal samples. These observations suggest that jailbreaks do not arise from a failure to recognize harmful intent. Instead, the visual modality shifts representations toward a specific jailbreak state, thereby leading to a failure to trigger refusal. To quantify this transition, we identify a jailbreak direction and define the jailbreak-related shift as the component of the image-induced representation shift along this direction. Our analysis shows that the jailbreak-related shift reliably characterizes jailbreak behavior, providing a unified explanation for diverse jailbreak scenarios. Finally, we propose a defense method that enhances VLM safety by removing the jailbreak-related shift (JRS-Rem) at inference time. Experiments show that JRS-Rem provides strong defense across multiple scenarios while preserving performance on benign tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/LeeQueue513/JRS-Rem.

### Title: Suppression of $^{14}\mathrm{C}$ photon hits in large liquid scintillator detectors via spatiotemporal deep learning
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.27727
Summary: arXiv:2603.27727v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Liquid scintillator detectors are widely used in neutrino experiments due to their low energy threshold and high energy resolution. Despite the tiny abundance of $^{14}$C in LS, the photons induced by the $\beta$ decay of the $^{14}$C isotope inevitably contaminate the signal, degrading the energy resolution. In this work, we propose three models to tag $^{14}$C photon hits in e$^+$ events with $^{14}$C pile-up, thereby suppressing its impact on the energy resolution at the hit level: a gated spatiotemporal graph neural network and two Transformer-based models with scalar and vector charge encoding. For a simulated test dataset in which each event contains one $^{14}\mathrm{C}$ and one e$^+$ with kinetic energy below 5 MeV, the models achieve $^{14}\mathrm{C}$ recall rates of 25%-48% while maintaining $\mathrm{e}^{+}$ to $^{14}\mathrm{C}$ misidentification below 1%, leading to a large improvement in the resolution of total charge for events where $\mathrm{e}^{+}$ and $^{14}\mathrm{C}$ photon hits strongly overlap in space and time.

### Title: Worst-Case Optimal $\sqrt{\epsilon}$-Rate Stability of KL Divergence under Perturbations of Gaussian Distributions
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.11026
Summary: arXiv:2604.11026v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: We establish a stability bound for the Kullback-Leibler (KL) divergence when its Gaussian reference distribution is perturbed. Existing relaxed triangle inequalities of KL divergence between Gaussian distributions require all three distributions to be Gaussian, which limits their use in applications such as out-of-distribution detection with flow-based generative models. Let $\mathcal{N}_1$ and $\mathcal{N}_2$ be $d$-dimensional Gaussian distributions, and let $P$ be any $d$-dimensional distribution satisfying $\mathbb{E}_P[\|X\|^2]<\infty$ and $\mathrm{KL}(P\Vert \mathcal{N}_1)<\infty$. If $\mathrm{KL}(\mathcal{N}_1\Vert\mathcal{N}_2)\leq\epsilon$ for $0<\epsilon<1/12$, we give an upper bound of $\left|\mathrm{KL}(P\Vert\mathcal{N}_1)-\mathrm{KL}(P\Vert\mathcal{N}_2)\right|$. Under suitable uniform boundedness conditions, the bound is $ \frac{\sqrt{6d\epsilon}}{2}+K\sqrt{\epsilon}+o(\sqrt{\epsilon})$ as $\epsilon\rightarrow 0$ for fixed $d$, where $K$ depends on the parameters of $\mathcal{N}_1$ and $\mathcal{N}_2$, and on $\mathbb{E}_P[\|X\|]$ and $\mathbb{E}_P[\|X\|^2]$. Furthermore, we prove that the $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ dependence is worst-case optimal. The result extends stability guarantees restricted to Gaussian triples to comparisons involving an arbitrary distribution and two nearby Gaussian references. Experiments on synthetic distributions and latent representations from flow-based models support the predicted stability behavior.

### Title: OmniTrace: A Unified Framework for Generation-Time Source Attribution in Omni-Modal LLMs
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.13073
Summary: arXiv:2604.13073v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: An omni-modal language model can answer a question about a multimodal document, a recording, or a video, but it cannot show which part of the input the answer came from. Asking the model to cite its evidence yields what it says it used; measuring what it used has so far required rerunning it many times with parts of the input removed, a cost that grows with every second of audio and video. We show that the generation pass already holds most of this information. OmniTrace attributes each generated sentence to source units (text chunks, images, and seconds of audio or video) by reading late-layer attention as mass over units with no extra forward pass and no per-modality tuning; head weights can be trained where ablation data are available. With fixed causal test setting, the units OmniTrace selects are causally important for responses and for individual sentences in video, audio, and image-text question answering on Qwen3-Omni-30B, MiniCPM-o 4.5 and Qwen2.5-Omni-7B. With one generation pass, OmniTrace recovers 77-96% of the effect of leave-one-out attribution, which needs up to 41 extra passes. OmniTrace also agrees best with human-marked evidence: its response-level rankings reach an AUROC of 0.83 on documents and 0.64 on video, against 0.76 and 0.60 for the strongest baseline.

### Title: Algorithmic Primitives of Theory of Mind Benchmarks
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17819
Summary: arXiv:2604.17819v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Do Theory of Mind (ToM) benchmarks really test theory of mind, or are they actually testing parsing and algorithmic state tracking? While benchmarks vary modality and dialogue length, this paper finds that they re-encode the same few state transition algorithms. We create PDDL-Mind, a neuro-symbolic approach that recovers such algorithms and upstream inductive biases of ToM benchmarks explicitly by parsing narratives into the Planning Domain Definition Language (PDDL) and executes candidate actions against a predefined set of update algorithms. Doing so provides program-verified state traces to the model. We use our method's performance gain to audit how much of a benchmark's difficulty our inductive bias absorbs. Across MMToM, MuMA, and FanToM, outsourcing parsing to programs alone brings performance close to strong agentic pipelines, while outsourcing state tracking to PDDL moves GPT-4o to close-to-human performance. We demonstrate that complex ToM benchmarks primarily punishes Large Language Models' unreliable implicit implementation of state tracking and parsing algorithms rather than limitations in belief and counterfactual reasoning.

### Title: Activation-Efficient Fine-Tuning via Sequence-Compressed Low-Rank Subspaces
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.22783
Summary: arXiv:2604.22783v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) has become the standard approach for adapting large language models (LLMs). However, despite reducing trainable parameter counts, many PEFT methods remain memory-bound by token-level activation intermediates that scale linearly with sequence length, limiting their practicality for long-context and on-device adaptation. Unlike prior memory-efficient PEFT approaches that preserve token-level adapter activations, LARS (Low-memory Activation-Rank Subspace) reduces the sequence-length scaling of stored adapter activation intermediates from O(BSRL) to O(BRL) by performing adaptation within a sequence-level low-rank subspace. Through pooled feature extraction, low-rank subspace modulations, and residual projection, LARS reduces the overall memory footprint by 23% on GPUs and 47.8% on CPUs relative to LoRA at S = 1024, with relative savings increasing with context length. Experiments across reasoning, understanding, long-context, retrieval, and token-level tasks show that LARS maintains competitive performance relative to LoRA while substantially improving memory efficiency. Beyond GPUs, LARS also reduces average CPU power and energy consumption per optimisation step by 22.43% and 22.06%, respectively. These results demonstrate that activation-efficient adaptation provides a practical path toward resource-constrained and on-device fine-tuning of LLMs.

### Title: CastFlow: Bridging Evidence Acquisition and Accurate Prediction in Agentic Time Series Forecasting
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27840
Summary: arXiv:2604.27840v3 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Time series forecasting is a fundamental task in temporal data analytics, and large language model (LLM)-based agents offer a promising paradigm through autonomous tool interaction and evidence-grounded reasoning. However, two fundamental challenges remain: how to acquire forecast-relevant evidence through adaptive tool interactions, and how to translate such evidence into accurate numerical predictions. To address these challenges, we propose CastFlow, a role-specialized agentic forecasting framework that bridges general-purpose tool reasoning and specialized temporal forecasting. Specifically, CastFlow introduces an Experience Memory that distills successful tool-use trajectories from training data, guiding a frozen LLM to orchestrate a multi-view toolkit for evidence acquisition. A lightweight specialized LLM then performs evidence-grounded temporal reasoning to refine an ensemble forecasting prior. To enhance this capability, we develop a two-stage training strategy combining supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, where final forecasting outcomes of the overall workflow guide the optimization of the specialized model. Our findings highlight the importance of bridging autonomous evidence acquisition and specialized temporal reasoning for accurate and adaptive agentic forecasting. The source code is available at https://github.com/Forever-Pan/CastFlow.

### Title: RamanBench: A Large-Scale Benchmark for Machine Learning on Raman Spectroscopy
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.02003
Summary: arXiv:2605.02003v4 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Machine learning (ML) has transformed many scientific fields, yet key applications still lack standardized benchmarks. We introduce RamanBench, the first large-scale, fully reproducible benchmark for ML on Raman spectroscopy, consisting of streamlined data access (https://pypi.org/project/raman-data/), evaluation protocols and code (https://pypi.org/project/raman-bench/), as well as a live leaderboard (https://huggingface.co/spaces/HTW-KI-Werkstatt/RamanBench). However, no single method dominates across these heterogeneous datasets, revealing that Raman spectroscopy remains an open challenge for machine learning. Therefore, we invite the community to contribute new approaches to our living benchmark, with the potential to accelerate advances in critical applications such as medical diagnostics, biological research, and materials science.

### Title: Learning reveals invisible structure in low-rank RNNs
Link: https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.04115
Summary: arXiv:2605.04115v2 Announce Type: replace-cross 
Abstract: Learning in neural systems arises from synaptic changes that reshape the representations underlying behavior. While low-rank recurrent neural networks (RNNs) have emerged as a powerful framework for linking connectivity to function, a theoretical understanding of their learning process remains elusive. Here, we extend the low-rank framework from activity to learning by deriving gradient-descent dynamics directly in a reduced overlap space. We formulate a closed-form, low-dimensional system of ODEs that governs learning in this space, exact for linear RNNs and asymptotically exact for nonlinear RNNs in the large-$N$ Gaussian limit. Central to our analysis is a distinction between two classes of overlaps: loss-visible overlaps, which fully determine network activity, output, and loss, and loss-invisible overlaps, which do not affect function but are required to describe learning. We illustrate the consequences of this decomposition through two phenomena. First, we show that learning can serve as a perturbation that exposes differences in connectivity between functionally equivalent networks. Second, we show that loss-invisible overlaps can act as memory variables that encode training history, and characterize the conditions under which this occurs. Finally, we present several testable predictions for biological learning experiments derived from our theory.
